Jeff Bezos once gave Eva Longoria and the admiral behind Osama bin Laden’s capture $100 million—but she says you don’t need wealth to give back
2026-10-31

Jeff Bezos once gave Eva Longoria and the admiral behind Osama bin Laden’s capture $100 million—but she says you don’t need wealth to give back

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
A lot of people talk about changing the world. But Amazon’s billionaire founder, Jeff Bezos, gave Eva Longoria $50 million to actually do it. And two years on, she says the biggest myth still surrounding philanthropy is that you have to be rich to do it. “One of the biggest misconceptions about philanthropy is that impact is tied to wealth or scale, when in reality, impact comes in many forms,” the Desperate Housewives star told Fortune. “What I’ve learned is that impact doesn’t come from how much money you have, it comes from how you show up.” It’s a message that feels pointed at a moment when billionaire philanthropy dominates headlines and ordinary people assume giving back is someone else’s job. For Longoria, the most powerful—and most overlooked—form of change happens at street level. “Mentoring someone, supporting small business, sharing what you know—those things matter because not everyone has access to capital or resources, but anyone can be part of someone else’s support system,” she adds. “And I think that’s really what’s missing for a lot of people. They don’t have someone in their corner.” The actress is practicing what she preaches. Having built a net worth north of $80 million thanks to a sprawling business portfolio—including a luxury tequila brand Casa Del Sol, the media company Hyphenate Media Group, a stake in women’s soccer team Angel City FC, and an early investment in the multibillion-dollar John Wick franchise. Now she’s channeling paying it forward by mentoring small-business owners through a new partnership with Lenovo. She’s also been running the Eva Longoria Foundation since 2012 to improve educational and entrepreneurial opportunities for Latinas in the U.S., as well as Eva’s Heroes, a charity that helps young adults with “intellectual special needs,” according to its website. Bezos and his wife, Lauren Sánchez Bezos, launched the Courage and Civility Award in 2021 for individuals who make significant contributions to society. They’ve given millions (often $100 million) to help those in need.  Previous recipients include CNN host and civil rights advocate Van Jones, chef and humanitarian José Andrés, and late country legend Dolly Parton—who famously helped fund Moderna’s COVID-19 vaccine research. It’s why Longoria says receiving the prize, alongside Bill McRaven, a retired Navy admiral, is much bigger than the money. “Receiving that support was incredibly meaningful, not just because of the scale of the gift, but because it represented trust and responsibility,” Longoria said, adding that she’s been “more intentional and more strategic” in how she shows up as an advocate. “This award has allowed me to continue investing those resources into the communities and causes I care deeply about—supporting Latina entrepreneurs, expanding access to education, and backing organizations that are driving real change on the ground,” she added. “The Courage and Civility Award makes it possible for us at the Eva Longoria Foundation to create long-term impact with transformative giving.” McRaven, who oversaw the 2011 raid that killed al Qaeda leader Osama bin Laden, pledged to use his share of the money to develop education programs for future military leaders and the children of deceased veterans, as well as mental health support for those who serve. Longoria isn’t the only one making the case that you don’t need to be rich and famous to make a difference. Just look at the Giving Pledge—the commitment, cofounded by Bill Gates, Melinda French Gates, and Warren Buffett, that asks the ultrawealthy to donate at least 50% of their fortunes—has attracted more than 250 signatories since its launch in 2010. The number who have actually followed through? A handful—fewer than 10. Most fulfilled the pledge only after their deaths. It’s why Liz Baker, CEO of Greater Good Charities, says we “can’t afford” to wait on billionaires to solve the world’s problems.:“I think if everybody did something to help in their community, we wouldn’t have the issues that we have.” The global nonprofit she runs has distributed more than $1 billion in impact across 121 countries since 2006—and she also told Fortune the idea that giving back has to be some grand, expensive gesture is one of the biggest things holding people back. “Everybody can chip in—and it doesn’t even have to be money. Like, do something,” she said. “Even if you’re like, I have one hour a week to solve this problem in my community that I care about,” Baker said. “Figure out how to do that. Most local nonprofits need help.” A version of this story originally published on Fortune.com on April 21, 2026. U.S workers just took home their smallest share of capital since at least 1947—and the lowest ever recorded
2026-10-31

U.S workers just took home their smallest share of capital since at least 1947—and the lowest ever recorded

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
As corporate earnings soar and U.S. GDP balloons, the American workforce isn’t feeling the same boom. American workers are taking home less of the country’s overall income, data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics shows, and employment in the U.S. is set to continue to slow. Labor share, or the portion of the U.S.’s economic output that workers receive through salary and wages, decreased to 52.8% in the second quarter of 2026, its lowest level since the BLS started recording this data in 1947, according to its labor productivity and costs report. In the previous quarter, labor share was at 53.6%. This decade, the labor share average was about 55.6%. That’s despite corporate earnings skyrocketing, with profits for Fortune 500 companies hitting a record $2.1 trillion in 2025. The U.S. GDP grew 3.4% in the third quarter last year, exceeding economists’ predictions. That growth has not only come at the expense of how much of the pie of wealth workers are taking home, but also how many Americans are in the workforce, economists warn. “That decline in the share of labor has got to be either falling earnings or falling numbers of people,” Raymond Robertson, a labor economist at Texas A&M’s Bush School of Government, told Fortune. “The falling share of income is having to do with the shift towards capital.” Indeed, there are growing signs that as national income balloons, the U.S. workforce is deflating. Unemployment ticked up to 4.2% in September, and employers added just 584,000 jobs in 2025 compared to 2 million added in 2024. The U.S. added 29,000 jobs in September, about one-third of economists’ projections. The bifurcation of corporate victories and weak labor data raises concerns among economists of jobless growth jeopardizing the U.S. workforce, as well as a K-shaped economy, where the rich get richer while the poor get poorer, becoming more exaggerated. “Data right now is very mixed,” Robertson said. “But I think it also all consistently points to this idea that things are getting worse for workers and much better for billionaires.” Robertson attributes weakening labor share averages to the rise in automation, which he noted is displacing workers, with productivity—a metric essentially measuring worker output—continuing to rise. Second-quarter GDP data showed nonfarm productivity growth grew 1.4% last quarter, though the rate has slowed from 2.2% in the same quarter a year ago. “All these things, bit by bit, are replacing people, and they’re concentrating income and their share of capital,” he said. Goldman Sachs analysts Joseph Briggs and Sarah Dong estimated in a report, based on Department of Labor job numbers, that AI automation could displace 25% of all work hours. They predicted that over the course of the AI adoption period, a 15% increase in AI-driven productivity would displace 6% to 7% of jobs, and, at its peak, a 1 million increase in unemployed workers, though job displacement as a result of AI has not shown up meaningfully in any economic data. The displacement could be substantial, the analysts said, but said the impacts of automation will be tempered by a wealth of new jobs created as a result of the technological changes. Automation is expected to be a boon to corporate profits and GDP, projected to boost GDP by 1.5% by 2035, according to a Wharton brief published in September 2025. Early signs indicate AI could drive productivity gains, with around 80% of employees reporting AI makes them faster at specific tasks, but only 37% of companies seeing an impact of that productivity on operating profits, according to McKinsey data, and AI operating costs continue to constrain its deployment. Robertson added that growing unemployment, which he expects to see rise over the next few months, keeps wages down, allowing margins and profits to expand. To be sure, the recent productivity surge has been an “open question,” Morgan Stanley economists have argued, not unanimously attributed to increased adoption of AI or automation. The analysts suggested this increase would be cyclical, or vestiges of pandemic-era habits of companies making more from less. An Oxford Economists research brief from earlier this year suggested companies are disguising overhiring-related layoffs as a result of AI, but said automation-related workforce reductions have not yet happened en masse. Additionally, while unemployment has been ticking up over the past year, it is still relatively low. Mark Regets, senior fellow at National Foundation for American Policy (NFAP), sees a different reason for a slowing workforce. He told Fortune President Donald Trump’s immigration crackdown has not done what Trump administration officials, such as White House Deputy Chief of Staff Stephen Miller, said it would in increasing the number of U.S.-born workers. Instead, according to Regets, Trump’s immigration policies have not only decimated the foreign-born workforce, but has also created fewer opportunities for domestic-born workers to find jobs. NFAP data shows a 1.1 million to 1.4 million-person reduction in the foreign-born workforce since January 2025, consistent with the Congressional Budget Office’s report last year indicating shrinking U.S. population growth as a result of migrants being deported or refusing to come to the U.S. out of fear of hostile polities. “The data is raising huge red flags that we are losing immigrants of all types that we otherwise would be advancing America’s economy,” Regets said. Recent BLS data is counter-evidence to Miller’s argument that harsher immigration policy would grow the U.S. workforce. The labor participation rate for foreign-born workers fell from 66.3% to 66% from September 2025 to September 2026, and labor participation for native-born workers has similarly fallen from 61.6% to 60.9% over the same period. Fewer immigrant workers may actually make it harder for U.S.-born individuals to find work. “A company unable to find the workers it needs for some roles could shut down operations rather than continuing,” Regets said. He noted skillset diversity in a workplace could boost productivity and justify employing more people. Greater immigration can also increase consumer spending and stimulate businesses, as well as encourage businesses to take advantage of ample labor market availability and seek out their labor instead of offshoring jobs. While friendlier immigration policies could help reverse an exodus of foreign-born workers, Robertson said addressing the workplace automation push would be key to growing the U.S. workforce. “There are trades that are technology-assisted,” he said. “Those are going to be in higher demand, but you really still have to have a significant investment in skills.” The young generation of workers are already prepared to adapt to a changing labor landscape. Gen Z are flocking to trade schools in hopes of a finding a job as a carpenter or welder not so easily outsourced by AI, and in 2024, enrollment in vocation-based community colleges increased 16%, according to data from the National Student Clearinghouse. Companies have taken it upon themselves to provide reskilling opportunities to employees. A CareerTrainer survey from June found that 72% of hiring managers intended to reskill employees. While the U.S. Department of Labor updated guidelines to encourage states to adapt workplace development systems, Robertson argued the government hasn’t done enough in several decades to imbue the workforce with necessary skillsets for future jobs. “Democrats and Republicans have not significantly invested in training [or] the retraining or active labor market programs that you need to match workers to jobs,” Robertson said. “That’s the obvious solution.” Without changes, economists see the pattern of an employment slowdown continuing, but with greater concern about the ability for the U.S. economy to sustain growth. “We need job growth to have a growing economy, and I think we need job growth to pay our debts,” Regets said. “I don’t know how you have job growth with a shrinking labor force.” A version of this story was published on Fortune.com on Jan. 13, 2026. This top data scientist’s (non-AI) model finds Republicans have just one chance in six of holding Senate
2026-10-31

This top data scientist’s (non-AI) model finds Republicans have just one chance in six of holding Senate

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
I started following Thomas Miller’s political forecasts during the January 2021 Senate races in Georgia. Just a few days before the election, the polls and pundits anticipated a relatively easy win for both Republican candidates. But Miller—a prominent data scientist and professor at Northwestern University—posited a Democratic sweep, and he was right. He also called Trump’s victory in 2024. What’s different about Miller’s models? They’re far more current than the polls, and they’re centered on prediction market prices that are typically a better guide than the voter surveys—plus Miller adjusts the outlook in every Senatorial contest by using all we know about every contest. Now, Miller is applying an updated version of his model for the current Senate elections. You can find the stats on his website, Virtualtout.io, and I highly recommend paying a visit. Once again, Miller starts by incorporating the betting venues’ data. “I use the numbers from two sites, Polymarket and Kalshi,” he told Fortune. “They’re the ones that trade the most dollars, where most of the action happens.” Miller then calibrates the posted “prices” so that they reflect probabilities, and the odds for both leading candidates tally to 100%. In a further step, he deploys multiple time series analysis to find underlying factors driving the betting prices, and uses trusted models from finance and economics to project how they’re likely to influence voters moving forward. By the way, he thinks his model works better than AI. Miller updates the probabilities of a Republican or Democratic win every 10 minutes. The odds that a candidate will win scroll across the top of the Virtualtout.io screen continuously, in a band resembling a stock ticker tape. For each race, Miller also posts the change over the last 24 hours. Miller calls the streaming feature the “NowCast.” As for those “underlying” factors, one force swamps all others: Donald Trump. “The states don’t move independently of one another,” says Miller. “If one goes up for Democrats, chances are they’ll all go up for Democrats, and vice versa. The entire market is dominated by what Trump does and says hour by hour.” Put simply, when voters don’t like the POTUS’s words or actions, all Republican odds tend to fall. Miller asserts that his model has identified 85% to 90% of what’s causing the variances on Kalshi and Polymarket, and “my interpretation is that it’s overwhelmingly Trump and the Republican positions generally.” Though he opened Virtuatout.io to public view about a week ago, Miller started running the numbers on September 17. What’s most striking is just how far the GOP chances have faded in just six weeks. As of around 9 AM on October 30, five days before most polling sites close, Virtualtout.io gives the Republicans a 17.1%, or just over one-sixth probability, of winning 50 seats and hence retaining control of the upper chamber. On September 17, that figure was twice as high at 34.8%, or slightly better than one-in-three. In all five contests where the Democrats can flip a seat, their chances are better than 60%—in most cases, much better. In North Carolina, where Republican Thom Tillis is retiring, leaving an open seat, the GOP was never remotely close, and now harbors a minuscule 4% shot at victory. In Texas, the Republican odds have dropped since September 17 from 46.15% to 31.9%, in Ohio from 45.4% to 31.8%, and in Alaska from 32.95% to 28.6%. In only one state is the GOP faring better than in mid-September and that’s Maine. Republican Susan Collins started as an underdog at 32.6%, but has clawed back to 39.9%, adding 1.7 points in the last 24 hours. In Alaska, things are also looking up for the Republicans: After falling to a low of 23.4% on October 23, incumbent Dan S. Sullivan’s prospects have improved by over 5 points to 28.6%. For the political pros looking for harbingers of big trouble for either side, the towering sign is the Republican woes in traditional strongholds Iowa and Kansas. In the former, Republican Ashley Hinton dropped from 61.75% on September 17 to a virtual tie at 53.4%, a trend mirrored in Kansas where Roger Marshall was looking a likely victor six weeks ago at 75.8%; now, he’s down almost 19 points at 57.1%. In the last day, Hinton and Marshall have shed 1.45% and 2.47 points respectively. In the one state where Republicans saw a big chance for a flip, the horizon’s receding fast. The GOP chances of beating Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan have slipped from better than a third in mid-September to about a quarter (25.4%). If the Democrats indeed score a flip in every contest where their probabilities in Miller’s model stand at 60% or better, and hold all the seats they have now, they’ll clinch a majority of 52 to 48, nearly reversing the Republican dominance at 53-47. In South Carolina, recently rerated on the Fox News Channel from “lean Republican” to “tossup,” Miller still has Darline Graham at a commanding 75%, though she’s slipped 2.4 points in the past 24 hours. Keep in mind that Miller’s setting odds, not making definitive predictions. A candidate at over 30% still stands a decent chance of winning. And Republicans are over that bogey in Maine, Ohio, and Texas, and close and trending the right way in Alaska. Odds-defying events happen all the time. The problem for Republicans is that they’d have to beat those odds multiple times, plus defend seats they never dreamed they’d have to defend. It’s still far from over. And one of the best places to get a plausible, non-AI driven snapshot from a data scientist who’s been doing this for six years, is that NowCast on Tom Miller’s Virtualtout.io. Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network
2026-10-30

Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Alongside Norway, it is Ireland’s first time being included in the pan-European network. Ireland has joined the European Laboratory for Learning and Intelligent Systems (ELLIS) network after officially launching ELLIS Unit Ireland. The new unit marks the first time Ireland has joined the network, as ELLIS welcomed other new units in Austria, France, Germany and fellow first-timer Norway. The network has 49 units across Europe and aims to propel the continent as a leader at the forefront of AI research. The units operate as localised hubs whereby researchers can work together, share ideas across a range of disciplines and carry out long-term research. The unit is part of Rinn AI, which is Ireland’s national centre for research and innovation in data science and AI and is funded by Research Ireland. Prof Noel O’Connor, Rinn AI’s director, will lead ELLIS Unit Ireland, which will focus on advancing fundamental AI research in core machine learning and related areas such as computer vision, natural language processing and information retrieval. The unit will also work closely with domain experts, policymakers and societal stakeholders, as a means of addressing real-world challenges. There will be a focus on connecting researchers across the country and training the next generation of ML and AI researchers. Commenting on Ireland’s inclusion to the network, O’Connor said: “Joining ELLIS is a significant moment for AI research in Ireland. Through Rinn AI, we have brought together researchers from every university in the country and ELLIS Unit Ireland now connects that community directly to Europe’s leading network in machine learning. “It opens new doors for collaboration, gives our researchers and students a platform on the European stage and helps ensure the AI we develop is shaped by the people it serves.” SiliconRepublic.com recently spoke with Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork, about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares
2026-10-30

Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
University of Bristol’s Prof Michelle Spear looks at how your brain could be responsible for things that go bump in the night, not ghosts. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) It’s three in the morning. You’ve woken from a dead sleep because you’ve heard a thud in the darkness or a floorboard creaking downstairs. Or perhaps you’ve woken because you have the unmistakable sensation you’re not alone. During daylight, these phenomena might be dismissed as the heating, your neighbours or the sounds of an old house settling. But in the middle of the night, they can feel a tad more sinister. Your mind might immediately start reeling, jumping to the worst-case scenario – wondering if you’ve been broken into, or if you’re being haunted by some supernatural force. In reality, the explanation is far more simple. Things really do go bump in the night. But occasionally, the culprit is closer to home than we might imagine. Falling asleep is not the neurological equivalent of switching off a light. The brain moves gradually from wakefulness into sleep, accompanied by changes in sensory processing, muscle activity and awareness of the world around us. But these changes don’t always occur in perfect synchrony. So for brief periods, characteristics of sleep and wakefulness can overlap. The results can be surprisingly spooky. If you’ve ever been on the verge of sleep only to feel that you’ve fallen off a kerb, missed a step or dropped through the bed, you’ve probably experienced a hypnic jerk. Also known as a sleep start, this is a sudden involuntary contraction of one or more muscles as we move into the earliest stages of sleep. It’s an example of how the transition between wakefulness and sleep isn’t always a smooth one. Hypnagogic experiences can also involve vivid sounds, images or sensations. So as you’re on the edge of sleep, you may think you hear footsteps or whispers – or you may even think you see a figure in the room. Exploding head syndrome – perhaps the most alarming name for a harmless sleep condition – is another example of how the boundaries between sleep and wakefulness can become blurred. As someone is falling asleep, or occasionally waking, they suddenly perceive an enormous noise: an explosion, gunshot or slamming door – yet nothing in the outside world has made a sound. Its precise cause remains uncertain. But the phenomenon demonstrates something important: hearing a sound and a sound actually occurring are not quite the same thing. The conscious experience of hearing is constructed by the brain. So neural activity generated without an external sound can still produce an auditory experience that seems entirely real. The brain doesn’t even need to be asleep for this to happen. If you’ve ever thought you’ve heard someone call your name – only to turn around and find no one there – you’ve been tricked by your brain. Another possible explanation for the sounds we think we hear at night involves source monitoring. This is the brain’s ability to distinguish events generated internally from those coming from the outside world. Thoughts, memories and imagined speech are internally generated, yet normally retain the quality of being ours. But occasionally, that attribution goes awry – allowing something generated within the brain to feel as though it came from somewhere else. Around sleep, that boundary becomes particularly interesting. Another common – but frightening – experience that can happen at night is waking up with the sudden feeling that someone else is in the room. This experience is usually the result of sleep paralysis. During rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, when most of our vivid dreaming occurs, the brain temporarily suppresses activity in most muscles. This protective mechanism, known as REM atonia, prevents us from physically acting out our dreams. But during sleep paralysis, our waking awareness returns while this physical paralysis persists. You know where you are, but cannot move. Yet elements of dream perception can remain active. This is why you may have experienced the sensation that someone – or something – is in the room with you, despite clear sensory evidence of the contrary. One clue as to why this happens comes from experiments examining how the brain distinguishes itself from others. In one study, participants controlled a robotic device positioned in front of them, while a second robot, which was placed behind them, reproduced these movements by touching the participants’ backs. But when researchers introduced a slight delay between the movement and the touch, some participants began to feel as though somebody else was touching them. Some even sensed another person standing behind them. While this experiment doesn’t explain every felt presence that can happen during sleep paralysis, it does reveal something remarkable: the distinction between ourselves and others is something the brain has to construct. Usually, movement, touch and our sense of body position agree about what belongs to us. But if that relationship is disrupted, an internally generated sensation can begin to feel like it originated elsewhere. Now picture this happening in a dark bedroom, when you’re partly awake yet still on the edge of dreaming and unable to move. The sensation that somebody else is there becomes rather less mysterious. Perception is not a passive recording of the world. It combines incoming sensory information with expectation and previous experience to construct the most likely explanation. But at the boundary between sleep and waking, this process can become less reliable. We may hear sounds that were never made, experience fragments of dreams intruding our bedroom or interpret sensations arising from our own bodies as the presence of somebody else. Michelle Spear is a professor of anatomy at the University of Bristol. Her research focuses on the architecture of synovial joints, drawing on her experience in histopathology to examine clinical and functional aspects of tissue-based research. Silicon Republic
Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland
2026-10-30

Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Prof Paul Townsend tells us about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Over the summer, seven new Research Ireland centres were launched to address key research areas deemed pertinent to the country’s scientific, medical, economic and technological development. The centres belong to a national research network called ‘Rinn’ and between them are set to receive €460m in Government investments over the next eight years, supporting 577 research positions, developing more than 800 PhDs and collaborating with 17 research-performing organisations. The seven Rinn research centres – dedicated to the fields of advanced therapies, AI, energy, medical devices, pharma and biopharma, quantum, and semiconductors – began their work in July, with each being led by directors who are experts in their sectors. Rinn Semiconductors is set to receive nearly €71m of Government funding overall and is headed by Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork (UCC) and with Tyndall National Institute who was born and grew up in the UK. “I studied physics at university, gaining a PhD from the University of Cambridge on the novel properties of polymers that behave as one-dimensional semiconductors,” he tells SiliconRepublic.com. His subsequent work in industry included time spent at British Telecom on quantum cryptography, the then-emerging concept of exploiting quantum physics to secure information. This work, he explains, was ancestral to “the quantum information and computing revolution that we frequently read about” today. “In 2003, I had the opportunity to move to Ireland with my family and colleagues to found a new group at UCC and Tyndall, working on optical fibre communication systems with funding from Science Foundation Ireland,” says Townsend. Tasked then with supporting the development of the wider photonics research ecosystem and engagement with industry in Ireland, this has since become the focus of his time working with Tyndall as its head of photonics research. “My ambition is to take this an exciting further step forward with Rinn Semiconductors,” says Townsend. The centre is set to focus on research and innovation in heterogeneously integrated semiconductor systems, and aims to lead Ireland’s response to the point at which traditional improvements in computer chips diminishes (the end of Moore’s Law scaling) by pioneering modular integrated circuit-based – or chiplet-based – semiconductor design and integration. Chiplet architecture is a method of using multiple smaller modules to build a single larger processor for a more powerful end result. The Rinn Semiconductors team will seek transformative advances in these technologies and to apply them in three main areas: future internet, sustainable energy and the environment, and digital healthcare. “Today, semiconductor chips touch nearly every aspect of our daily lives; they are in phones, computers and medical technology,” notes Townsend. “Almost any problem you can think of – societal or technological – requires the use of semiconductors to provide a solution. “This is also a technology where Ireland plays a leading role. We are home to a world-class semiconductor industry sector – with a 50-year operating history – that employs 20,000 people in more than 100 companies. “The national semiconductor strategy, Silicon Island, aims to build upon this to position Ireland internationally as a hub of semiconductor excellence and to contribute to the EU Chips Act goal of growing the EU’s proportion of global semiconductor manufacturing output.” Key elements of the Government’s Silicon Island strategy, he notes, include strengthening research capacity, supporting start-ups and spin-outs, and building a future-ready talent pipeline through education and skills programmes. “Rinn Semiconductors is extremely well-positioned to make major contributions to the achievement of these goals,” he says. Another “key objective” of the centre, according to Townsend, is “to foster deep collaboration across the seven universities and institutions involved” by leveraging the “world-class” semiconductor fabrication facilities and infrastructure at Tyndall. The centre plans for what it calls “radical collaboration” with and between Dublin City University, Munster Technological University, Trinity College Dublin, Tyndall, UCC, University of Galway and University of Limerick. This, it is hoped, will lead to “a cohesive ‘atom to systems’ value chain in Ireland, connecting everything from basic materials research through to complete technology solutions”, says Townsend. The Rinn Network, in his view, offers the opportunity to leverage coordinated research at scale, which is “critical” to the development of complex technologies such as semiconductors. “The significant scale of the industry collaborative research projects we have established in just the first two months of operation gives me great confidence that this vision can be realised.” As centre director, Townsend is responsible for ensuring that his programme is “suitably prioritised, innovative, impactful, internationally leading in its focus areas and, critically, delivers on its mission and goals”. He is also in charge of budgeting, and overseeing grant compliance and reporting. “In addition, I take the lead on stakeholder and external relations management, and act as an advocate for the centre and its mission,” he says. Another important part of his leadership role is “to ensure the centre is a beacon for attracting and developing talent at all levels from PhD students to senior principal investigators (PIs)” in order to “address the demand from industry for highly-skilled scientists and engineers, as well as to sustain the academic research ecosystem needed to supply these trainees and keep Ireland at the forefront of semiconductor research”. Apart from the development and implementation of the centre’s scientific and operational strategy, Townsend – whose personal research background is in optical fibre communications and sensing – gets some hands-on time at Rinn Semiconductors, too. “I am also an active PI in the centre, where my group focuses on developing new integrated semiconductor solutions for two main application areas: energy-efficient interconnect technologies for AI clusters in data centres, with the goal of constraining the overall power consumption of AI; and distributed acoustic sensing in optical fibre cables, where we target improved monitoring of undersea communication cables and offshore windfarm power cables to ensure resilience and protection.” Moving forward, Townsend says his Rinn centre is targeting progress in four main areas: research breakthroughs in semiconductor chiplet integration and packaging technologies; translation of these ideas into new manufacturable technologies and products; acting as a catalyst for talent development; and driving engaged research programmes with stakeholders for impact on policy and society. The latter, he hopes, could include “addressing the early stages of the talent supply problem by working with teachers and policymakers on how to encourage more school and university students, particularly women, to take up physics and engineering subjects that can lead to a career in the semiconductor industry”. Townsend notes, too, the potential of the overall network for fostering collaboration and shared progress across the seven cornerstone areas, and the prospect of creating “greater value and impact than could be achieved by a group of individual centres working in a less connected way”. “The wider Rinn Network plan adds the exciting possibility for centres to work together on complex problems that can only be addressed through an interdisciplinary approach. “I think the scale and ambition of the overall programme will also be a great attractor for external talent and a fantastic springboard for researchers already in Ireland to advance their careers. An especially important goal to him on an individual level, he adds, would be “the development of the next generation of exciting, talented researchers who will take Ireland on to even greater success in the future”. “I am very fortunate and proud to have had the opportunity to work in Ireland and to contribute at least somewhat to the development of the national research ecosystem. “On the personal side, my wife and I and our two now-grown-up daughters all live happily in and around Cork – and wouldn’t want to be anywhere else.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal
2026-10-30

Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Jocelyn Bell Burnell. Image: Chris Bellew, Fennell Photography The astrophysicist has a long legacy of scientific research, discovery and supporting the wider STEM community. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell has been awarded the UCD Ulysses Medal for her outstanding scientific achievements and ongoing commitment to developing the next generation of researchers. The Ulysses Medal is awarded to those who have contributed something of immense importance on a global scale. Previous recipients include author Margaret Atwood, former Irish President Mary McAleese and the ‘Godfather of AI’, Prof Geoffrey Hinton. Raised in Northern Ireland, Burnell previously described her early STEM education as challenging. Despite her assurance that she wanted to study the stars surrounding our planet, her gender precluded her from engaging with STEM subjects in school, as they were aimed primarily at young men. She persevered, becoming one of only three young girls taking science in her secondary school, where she topped the class in physics and then continued on to the University of Glasgow and then Cambridge, where she undertook a PhD. It was at Cambridge where she worked on the development of a radio space telescope designed to explore the furthest reaches of space and where she discovered a “scruff” on one of the charts she was analysing. This was the moment she identified what would become known as a pulsating radio star, or pulsar. Despite her work however, Burnell was notably excluded when the discovery was recognised by the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1974. The prize was instead awarded to her PhD supervisor, Antony Hewish and a fellow Cambridge professor, Martin Ryle. Her exclusion stands as a prime example of how the achievements of women were so often disregarded in favour of their male co-workers and supervisors. In 2018, she donated £2.3m to start the Bell Burnell Graduate Scholarship Fund, a fund supporting students coming from underrepresented backgrounds. The money was part of the Special Breakthrough Prize in Fundamental Physics that she had earned for her previous discovery and five decades of leadership in the field. Prof Lorraine Hanlon , a full professor of astronomy at UCD, said Burnell had been “peerless in how she has deployed her brilliance, transforming her groundbreaking discovery into lifelong advocacy for curiosity-driven research, equity and global science diplomacy. “The achievements of all the Bell Burnell scholars stand as living proof of Dame Jocelyn’s enduring legacy in recognising and empowering the next generation of scientific leaders.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. OpenAI Sam Altman says sorry, mathematicians: the onslaught of AI math slop will continue. But this doesn’t seem to bother Altman all that much. In an interview on the Cheeky Pint podcast on Thursday, he acknowledged the disruption that OpenAI was causing to mathematicians, but said that the work must continue — for the good of humankind, he argued. “I think, on net, the world deserves to understand how the universe works,” Altman said the podcast appearance, as spotted by The Verge. “But I do have empathy for the method.” According to the outlet’s reporting, it’s the first time that Altman has directly acknowledged his company’s impact on mathematicians. OpenAI’s explosive foray into math started when it claimed to have found a solution to the famous Navier-Stokes problem by using 10,000 AI agents that worked 88 hours straight. A mathematician accused OpenAI of unfairly piggy backing off his unreleased research and threatening to ruin his career when he said he told them he would go public with his accusations. Prominent names in the field, meanwhile, began to worry about the potential new paradigm that OpenAI had potentially introduced: wealthy AI companies could now jump on someone else’s ongoing research and beat them to the finish line by throwing ungodly amounts of AI computing power at the problem, and then take credit for it; OpenAI had spent around $10 million just to come up with its Navier-Stokes solution. But that was just the beginning. Soon, it released hundreds of more AI-generated mathematical proofs, a move that was blasted by top mathematicians and even a math advisory group that OpenAI had been consulting with. Critics note how none of the papers have been vetted or reviewed, not least of all because the natural language proofs are borderline incomprehensible. It will take years for mathematicians to unpack all the AI-generated proofs OpenAI has dumped on their laps, while OpenAI, in the here and now, gets to use them as purported proof of their technology being revolutionary. On the podcast, Altman admitted that “we did let it get it out of control for one week on math,” but justified the endeavor because of the importance of what they were finding. “I feel unequivocal on this point: Humanity deserves fundamental knowledge, and we should not be sitting on a major fundamental science discovery,” he said. “The world deserves that.” More on AI: Pope Leo Comes Out Swinging Against AI Personhood

It’s a leader in the West, but it’s playing catch up with Chinese competitors, who altogether already run more than 90 humanoid data collection centers, according to data from the consulting firm Interact Analysis, while Europe only has ten. Currently, most robots used in factories are specialized for specific tasks. Humanoid robots aim to be versatile like a real employee, but are harder to pull off. There’s still huge questions over their, but the idea seems to making some headway, with BMW preparing to trial robots from the Swiss company Hexagon Robotics on the factory floor. Japan Airlines is also experimenting with robot baggage handlers at Tokyo’s Haneda airport, who presumably have had plenty of time to hit the gym. Back in May, the US Coast Guard announced a plan to deploy autonomous naval drones across the five Great Lakes. As Mike Bradley, mayor of the Ontario town of Sarnia told the CBC, the naval drones look “like something from Mars.” Bradley says he first laid eyes on one of the Trump administration’s new surveillance boats as it sailed up the St Claire River, a natural border connecting Lake Huron with Lake St Clair, a minor body of water just east of Detroit. That being the case, the US Coast Guard should have had ample time to inform their Canadian counterparts of the planned operations — though whether there was any communication about the sail drones at all remains an open question. Psychedelic Compound in Toad Venom Can Treat Depression When Snorted, Scientists Find

Visit Original Website
In the late 1980s and early 90s, sensational reports of a “toad licking” fad spread like wildfire throughout North and South America. The stories — aided in no small part by harrowing advisories from the US Drug Enforcement Administration — told of an epidemic of teens licking toads in order to ingest compounds secreted by the animals, most notably bufotenine, an alkaloid compound with psychedelic effects. How widespread this phenomenon actually was in practice is difficult to say. Contemporary media analysis found that tales of rampant toad-tonguing spread through a game of academic telephone, which was quickly picked up by mainstream media. As all media frenzies do, the supposed drug epidemic quickly fell off the radar, and psychedelic toads became something of an obscure proverb known mainly to drug researchers. Decades later, however, ongoing research into the substances secreted by the amphibians seems to have led to an actual breakthrough, in the form of a single-dose nasal spray meant to treat severe cases of depression. As ScienceAlert reports, researchers at the Eli Lilly subsidiary AtaiBeckley recently concluded clinical trials around mebufotenin, one of the active psychedelic compounds found in the venom of the Sonoran Desert toad. Per their findings, published in the Journal of the American Medical Association, doses of mebufotenin spray demonstrated “potential as an effective, well-tolerated therapy for treatment-resistant depression with a rapid recovery time.” The findings come via a double-blind randomized clinical trial involving 193 adults with treatment-resistant depression, which is characterized by the Cleveland Clinic as major depressive disorder which persists following at least two different antidepressant treatments. Overall, the researchers found that eight and 12 milligram doses of toad spray resulted in significantly reduced scores on a standard depression test, with only mild to moderate side-effects. Even more promising is the fact that those positive effects held for weeks after a single dose. “Single-day dosing with mebufotenin appears to be effective for postpartum depression — and now for difficult-to-treat depression — and the benefits are sustained with or without re-treatment for eight days to six months,” psychopharmacology researcher Chittaranjan Andrade told ScienceAlert. Still, Andrade isn’t convinced that toad secretions are the holy grail of antidepressants, at least based on this clinical trial, which — while being the largest study yet of mebufotenin as a nasal spray — still has key limitations. “The average patient became only about 30 percent better,” Andrade explained in his own editorial, “and the response and remission rates were below those typically seen, for example, with ketamine and electroconvulsive therapy.” “Patients with difficult-to-treat depression require long-term maintenance treatment,” Andrade continued, “but because mebufotenin was examined as monotherapy, the trial offers no insight on when and how the transition to a conventional antidepressant should be made or whether mebufotenin itself might be used for maintenance.” More on treatments: Chinese Scientists Develop Muscle Grafts That Can Be Injected Straight Under the Skin

Creeps Are Using AI to Undress Celebrities in Paparazzi Shots for Clicks
2026-10-31

Creeps Are Using AI to Undress Celebrities in Paparazzi Shots for Clicks

Futurism | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Engagement hackers on X-formerly-Twitter have a new strategy to attract attention: use AI to undress celebrity men and publish it as if it’s the real deal. The strategy, which was flagged earlier this week by writer Sarah Hagi, is pretty simple. Posters take existing paparazzi photos of celebrity men and alter them so that the men are shirtless and, most often, extremely jacked. They then republish the synthetic images as if they’re new and real. The undressing is more than likely nonconsensual, making the whole scheme pretty gross. One frequent target is Callum Turner, the British actor and husband of pop star Dua Lipa. An account called “UKViewTIme” has posted several different AI-altered photos of Turner and Lipa together on the streets of NYC. Lipa, for her part, is seen completely clothed in pants and long jackets — while Turner is unnecessarily strolling about shirtless for no clear reason. Original paparazzi shots show that Turner was clothed in real life. Another recent post, published by an account called “BiTheWay,” show images of the “Stranger Things” star Noah Schnapp walking with the actor Sheridan Townsley. In the altered image published by “BiTheWay,” both of the young men are inexplicably pictured shirtless, however the original paparazzi images reveal that both Schnapp and Townsley were indeed wearing shirts and jackets. The culprits are going to extreme lengths to preserve the alleged authenticity of their inspiration. For instance, they’re going as far as to ensure that pop sensation Harry Styles’ famous tattoos are intact in AI-manipulated, topless images. “UKViewTime” was the culprit once again. Another repeat target has been Knox Jolie-Pitt, the son of Angelina Jolie and Brad Pitt. We also found one manipulated image of Pitt himself, who, despite wearing a yellow suit in the original photos, was altered to be shown in a black wife beater that reveals artificially-jacked arms. The deepfakes can be hard to spot because most of the images are in large part real. But if you find yourself wondering why Dua Lipa is dressed in an ankle-sweeping coat while her husband is out sans shirt, the answer is likely because you’re looking at a reality skewed by AI. OpenAI’s latest funding round isn’t quite living up to expectations, according to multiple news reports. OpenAI was previously reported to be raising $30 billion at a $1.4 trillion valuation, and then at a $1.25 trillion valuation, ahead of a planned IPO. But The Financial Times reported Friday that the company has subsequently lowered its funding target to $20 billion, with a first close that’s expected to be even lower than that. The round will be led by a group of investors from the United Arab Emirates, the FT said. A subsequent retail offering for wealthy individuals could bring the total funding amount to the $20 billion target. A follow-up story in The Wall Street Journal offered more details, namely that the company has secured $11 billion so far, and that its fundraising efforts had met with a “tepid” response from some large investors. TechCrunch has reached out to OpenAI for comment. The company’s reported funding challenges could be blamed, in part, on bad timing, as Wall Street investors have become wary of heavy AI infrastructure spending, leading to a major sell off of tech stocks. And Anthropic’s planned IPO could be facing challenges, too. From restaurant guides to payments, here’s how TikTok is building a super app
2026-10-31

From restaurant guides to payments, here’s how TikTok is building a super app

TechCrunch | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. TikTok announced this week that it’s adding an in-app restaurant guide, marking another step in the platform’s expansion beyond social media. The company has also recently been spotted developing a feature that would allow users to send money over direct messages. The social media app has steadily expanded its offerings in recent years, adding TikTok Shop, a map for local discovery, more robust search features, games, and more. It appears that TikTok is taking steps to evolve into a “super app,” a single platform where users can do much more than just watch and share videos and that can actually handle a wide range of needs in one destination. The super app model is big in China with apps like WeChat, which is kind of like Facebook, WhatsApp, Apple Pay, and an app store all rolled into a single platform. Of course, there’s the question of whether a super app model would work outside of China, but that doesn’t mean TikTok won’t try. Instead of switching between apps, TikTok is working to become the app that people use for most of their digital activities. After taking its biggest leap with TikTok Shop, the company — which, notably, transitioned to new, primarily U.S. ownership back in January — has applied the same playbook to recent developments. TikTok has become a popular destination for restaurant recommendations, with viral videos often driving customers to local eateries. Now, the platform is looking to make that discovery process even easier with a new feature called “Must Eat.” The community-driven guide highlights restaurants getting attention on the platform. “Must Eat” draws on more than 40 million posts and comments to compile restaurant recommendations and curated lists in one place. Users can browse restaurants by city and narrow their search by cuisine, price, rating, and location. The move puts TikTok in more direct competition with platforms like Yelp and Google Maps. It also reflects how the app is expanding its role in users’ everyday decisions, from what they buy to where they choose to go out to eat. Bloomberg reported in August that TikTok is developing a feature that would allow users to send each other money via direct messages. If rolled out, the feature would use the social media service’s TikTok Pay offering, which is already available in Southeast Asia for TikTok Shop purchases. Additionally, Reuters reported in March that TikTok had applied to Brazil’s central bank for approval to operate as a financial technology company offering lending and payment services. TikTok’s potential expansion into payments marks a significant way that the company is branching out beyond a social media platform and into a digital ecosystem. By aiming to bring financial services into its app, TikTok is looking to increase user engagement, open new revenue streams, and position itself to compete with fintech startups and e-commerce platforms. Over the years, TikTok has steadily been looking to become a place where users can come for sports highlights and content. In early June, the company launched a dedicated hub for the FIFA World Cup where users can see scores, match schedules, standings, trending videos, highlights, videos from players, and more. If people are already watching videos on TikTok but want to keep up with the score, they can do so without leaving the app or opening a dedicated sports news app or Google. The World Cup hub was made possible through TikTok’s Sports-focused product called “TikTok GamePlan,” which is designed to allow sports teams, leagues, and broadcasters to drive discovery and engagement on the platform. The social media giant also has partnerships with Major League Soccer (MLS) and Major League Baseball (MLB) for behind-the-scenes and exclusive content. In May, TikTok launched TikTok GO, a way for users to discover and book hotels, attractions, and experiences directly within its app in the United States. TikTok GO surfaces lodging and things to do through videos, search, and location pages. When users find something they’re interested in, they can view details, check availability, and complete a booking. Instead of directing users to third-party websites after they find a destination or recommendation in a video, TikTok has started positioning itself as a one-stop platform where viral travel content can drive bookings and revenue. While people have already been using TikTok as a search engine and replacement for Google, this latest step puts TikTok in more direct competition with Google’s core businesses, Search and Google Maps, because it’s working to not only be the app where you discover places, but also the platform where you purchase that trip. It’s widely known that one of TikTok’s biggest leaps beyond social media was the launch of TikTok Shop. TikTok began testing TikTok Shop in 2021 and launched it in the U.S. in 2023. Since then, the company has been able to successfully compete with Amazon, Shein, and other online marketplaces. According to eMarketer, TikTok Shop grew its U.S. sales by 407% in 2024 and another 108% in 2025 to reach $15.82 billion. As of last year, the company accounted for 18.2% of total social commerce in the U.S., with that number expected to reach 24.1% by 2027. Additionally, TikTok began challenging digital marketplaces even further with the launch of TikTok Shop gift cards late last year. TikTok Shop has also recently expanded into luxury retail, after initially mainly being known for cheap goods. TikTok’s popularity has influenced the music industry and how people discover new music, and the company tried to capitalize on this by launching a streaming service called TikTok Music in 2023 to take on platforms like Spotify and Apple Music, but ended up shutting it down a year later. The company said it would focus on driving music listening and continue partnering with music streaming services rather than competing with them. TikTok hasn’t completely abandoned its music ambitions, however, as the company recently introduced a feature that lets Apple Music subscribers play full songs in the app after discovering them on their “For You” feed. TikTok has launched a robust search experience that surfaces maps, local hashtags, and even reviews to help users discover trending restaurants, travel locations, shops, and local experiences. It has also added more detailed information about places and restaurants on dedicated pages, allowing users to quickly see things like opening hours, star ratings, price ranges, and more. TikTok was already eating into Google’s Search business when it first launched, as it quickly surfaced videos featuring commentary and visuals of restaurant food and places. However, users may still have needed to turn to Google Search to find a place’s exact location or to read reviews. Over the past few years, however, TikTok has increasingly eliminated that need by integrating detailed information about places right within its app. While TikTok is known for user-generated entertainment, the company has also begun embracing microdramas by launching an in-app Minis section and a dedicated stand-alone app for bite-sized TV shows that can be watched in a series of one-minute episodes. While TikTok already competes with streaming giants like Netflix for users’ attention, its move into scripted shows puts it in even more direct competition with them. It’s worth noting that TikTok first took strides into entertainment with the launch of livestreaming and support for longer videos, a significant move away from its initial focus on 15-second videos. TikTok has also launched a series of casual games in its app to get users to spend even more time on its platform and engage with others in DMs. The addition of games marked TikTok’s ongoing effort to go from a social media platform to an all-in-one entertainment platform where you can not only scroll through videos, but also challenge friends to easy-to-play games. I took two iPhones on vacation. The Duo won me over.
2026-10-31

I took two iPhones on vacation. The Duo won me over.

TechCrunch | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. “I think I’m going to switch back to my other iPhone.” I’ll admit, I said this multiple times during my five-day vacation with the new iPhone Duo, after encountering frustrations with broken apps, unfamiliar gestures (a return to Touch ID, argh!), and camera concerns that had me lugging around two phones. By the time the trip was over, I didn’t want to go back. When I first unboxed the Duo, a loaner from Apple, I was genuinely intrigued. I felt a flutter of anticipation as I peeled back the factory seal wrap and booted up the device. In retrospect, this was probably the first signal that Duo was going to ultimately win me over. As a tech reporter, I’ve received a lot of test devices over the years, so unfortunately, opening a package with a new gadget inside is no longer the thrill it used to be. But sometime in the days leading up to the Duo’s ship date, I found myself actually wanting one – I had started doing the math and comparing upgrade options that would allow me to afford the $2K the phone would cost. I started scrolling TikTok reviews of Duo cases. I hesitated not only because of the price but because moving to the Duo is a major change – especially coming from an iPhone 17 Pro Max. The phones could not be more different. As I was about to head out on vacation when the Duo arrived, I decided to tote my 17 Pro Max with me too, as something of a safety net. I ended up using the Duo more, and grew comfortable with it over the course of the trip, despite some frustrations. There’s always a psychological switching cost in changing phones, but particularly going from a high-end phone to a more experimental device like the Duo. For someone used to the iPhone’s long-perfected tech and thin rectangular shape, the Duo’s short, squat, and decidedly novel nature was somewhat off-putting at first. “Where is all that screen real estate I used to have?” I lamented. “Will this thing even fit comfortably in my pocket?” As it turns out, it does. Front and back pockets, in fact. Even while wearing a case. You can sit down with it in your back pocket, effectively squashing it, and it does not break. (I meant to be more careful, but I forgot to be, repeatedly, as I tend to carry phones in my back pocket when walking around. I sat on the phone countless times. It’s clearly not that fragile.) I also dropped the phone (by accident!) at least three times. Twice from about table height and once when it got pushed off the side of the bed. It did not fall on soft carpet, but rather on concrete, tile, and wood floors. It didn’t break then either, and the screen didn’t crack. I wouldn’t call these professional drop tests by any means, but it certainly was a relief for someone fairly clumsy with their phone. Of course, I did encounter some frustrations. I had to scroll more to read back through text threads because of the Duo’s small outer screen; unoptimized apps with numerous controls sometimes had key features tucked away in a “more” (three-dot) menu, leading to a hunt to find the option you needed; and, on multiple occasions, the Google Search app just froze. Though not Apple’s fault, the latter turned out to be my biggest annoyance software-wise, given that Search is one of my go-to apps especially when traveling. On vacation, I open the app repeatedly to look up points of interest, restaurant reviews, distances between towns, weather, and more. But Google’s app would just stop working, and tapping its back button did nothing. This seemed to be a problem specific to the Search app’s AI Mode, which these days you’re often dropped into without actually requesting it. (Even more reason to be annoyed by AI Search!) The workaround has been to force-close the app, so the next time you open it, your returned to the home screen with the search box. (It amazes me that, in a time when Google is facing a search decline because of AI, it wouldn’t have prioritized making its Search app functional on Apple’s latest device!) In addition, many of the early reviews pointed out how many apps were not updated or redesigned for Duo at launch, which meant they had a telltale black bar on the side, cramping the limited screen real estate when the phone is closed even further. This is true, but personally speaking, I didn’t think it was a dealbreaker for making a switch to the Duo. For one thing, companies have been pushing out their Duo-optimized updates fairly quickly. Those that have not – and there are still more than a few! – continue to be usable. They’re just kind of … ugly. Google Maps was not Duo-optimized during the trip, yet I had no problem using it for driving directions, local search and discovery, route planning, and the like. If anything, the black-barred apps felt more like an aesthetic issue than a functional problem, even though they’re smaller than usual when the Duo is folded. I expect all the major apps will eventually update. As for the older apps that never do? Well, it’s probably time to move on from apps no longer being maintained, I’d say, particularly in an era when older software is being exploited left and right by AI-enabled hacking tools. However, I will agree with the other reviewers who complained about the lack of Face ID. This is the Duo’s biggest drawback – not the screen, not the camera, not the lack of an Action button for launching your preferred AI assistant. It’s Touch ID. It is much easier to look at your phone than to fiddle around with finger presses. While I easily avoided the need to use Touch ID to unlock the device – I am almost always wearing an Apple Watch, which serves as an alternative authentication method – I often stumbled when it came to using apps and Apple Pay. Tap-and-pay is one of the areas where it’s immediately obvious how much smoother Face ID has made everyday tasks. Returning to Touch ID at the time of payment was cumbersome, given that you’re often holding shopping bags, a purse, and, in my case, a dog leash, in addition to the phone you’re using to pay with. There’s a certain amount of what I guess I’ll call “muscle memory” that you have to overcome when you switch back to Touch ID. I found the easiest grip was to cradle the phone in your hand and use your thumb to authenticate. But where I repeatedly messed up was remembering to keep my thumb or finger on the Touch ID sensor long enough to authenticate. At times, my finger wasn’t situated quite right (I blame the dog pulling on the leash!), and I had to readjust. “I miss Face ID!,” I complained multiple times at checkout. Though returning to Touch ID was something I got used to, it’s far from ideal. I can see this as being a real stopping point for some early adopters who would otherwise want to move to the Duo. It’s not an insurmountable problem, but it’s among the top reasons not to get the Duo now, in my opinion. (A future version is said to support Face ID.) Another reason to be wary of the Duo? The camera. Much has been said about the Duo’s lesser camera system. The Duo has two rear cameras to my iPhone 17 Pro Max’s three. It has no telephoto lens. It has a 10x digital zoom versus the Pro’s 40x. The Pro has more advanced hardware for low-light photography and supports ProRAW, ProRes, and other advanced features. Very simply put: if you are an iPhone photographer or a content creator, you shouldn’t consider the Duo as your primary device. For, you know, us normal folks, the Duo’s camera is okay. Not great, but fine. What were you going to do with those photos you’re snapping anyway? Post them to Instagram Stories or Facebook? Text them to a friend? Look back on them fondly when they reappear in your photo widget on your Home Screen? Turn them into a Lock Screen wallpaper? None of these things requires a Pro-level camera. Below are a few photos shot with the Duo versus others shot with the 17 Pro Max. None of the photos are using any special settings or filters; they are intentionally just average, non-professional point-and-shoot shots. Among my complaints are that the Duo’s image processing can, at times, make fine details, like the leaves on trees, look overprocessed. Meanwhile, distant landscapes can appear flatter or smoothed over — though, to be fair, the Blue Ridge Mountains are naturally hazy, so it’s hard to say how much of that is the camera. (Shots from the mountaintop were in low-light conditions — it was getting dark out.) As for why to get the phone? This one’s easy; it’s a foldable. From Apple. First, foldables are whimsical in a way that slab phones will never be. They are toys for grown-ups!  Developers are also clearly having fun building for the new format, so there are lots of new apps to play with. Plus, it’s a great way to watch movies, particularly with that Duo Folio case with a kickstand. Second, it’s a foldable from Apple. The company has taken its time to get it right, which comes through in the details, from the barely visible crease to the consistent aspect ratio between screens, allowing content to scale proportionally. It’s also a foldable in Apple’s software ecosystem, which matters for those who move between Apple devices and services and who use and pay for apps that are designed for iOS specifically. But here’s a small confession: in day-to-day life, I didn’t unfold the Duo as much as I thought I would. (To be clear: I absolutely did unfold it! I just thought I would do it more!) There are two reasons for this. One: unfolding the device in public invites conversation, and it feels sort of like a flex; I’m not always in a place where I have time to chat. I expect this problem to diminish in time as the novelty wears off. That said, I still had roughly half a dozen conversations with strangers while I was on vacation, from fellow shoppers to servers to business owners. I even handed them the device at times (when they couldn’t easily run off with it!) and let them fold and unfold it. One person wanted to see the Kid Cue feature in particular, which displays playful Snoopy animations on the phone’s outer display to get children to look toward the camera when you take their picture. They were genuinely delighted by this. Anecdotally, the consensus was that the unfolded screen was something of a marvel to most: “You can’t see the hinge!” “Is that a screen protector or is it just like that?” (The inner screen has a matte, nano-texture finish, reducing glare. Once explained, it was also praised as “pretty cool.” ) “What if you get sand in it?,” a former Samsung Galaxy Fold owner asked. Their older Android foldable had been ruined after a trip to the beach where sand got into the hinge, causing damage to the screen. (This was not uncommon at the time.) The Duo is rated IP68, the “6” signifying higher dust resistance, making it dust-tight. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 was rated IP48, by comparison. I’m not expecting it will have the same issues, though I do hope a reviewer takes it to the beach soon! I am certain that at least one of the handful of strangers I spoke to had never actually seen a phone that folded before. The fact that Apple has now made one means this concept will no longer be niche; it will be known to the mainstream. The other reason I didn’t unfold it as much as I thought is that doing so feels like the start of a more intentional screen time session. When I do unfold the phone now, it’s because I’m truly engaging with what I’m doing on the phone, like watching a show on Netflix or dragging two apps side-by-side in split-screen mode for multitasking. (I love reading news in Inoreader and having X open off to the side to see what people are saying about the news, but that’s a use case that won’t apply to everyone!) The split-screen feature is easy to use, too – with the app open, you swipe up from the bottom, then drag it to the left or right edge. Target boxes on each side help you visualize where it can move to, which is helpful. (You can also long-press on an app icon to open a split view, but I don’t do this as much.) But as something of a “mini” phone in terms of screen size when closed, I’m somehow less drawn to what I think of as time-wasting apps like TikTok, Instagram, or casual games. I’ve been thinking about this, and I don’t believe it’s just the lack of screen real estate when the phone is folded, or the fact that vertical apps haven’t been designed for the Duo’s unfolded landscape screen. Those concerns make sense from a practical, usability standpoint, of course, but there’s something more to this behavioral change, I believe. For years, phones have been getting bigger, taller, thinner, and more comfortable to hold. Consequently, they easily sucked us into their digital worlds. With the Duo, the design seems to interrupt the way smartphones effortlessly pull us in, begging us to open apps and scroll endlessly. Rather, the Duo feels like a device you open and use when you have reason to do so, not something you reflexively turn to when you’re bored or have a few minutes to spare. Because the best app use cases are when the phone is unfolded, and unfolding is something you do with careful consideration (and careful hands!), device use becomes more thoughtful. This is not a bad thing. It also aligns with the embrace of retro tech already underway, where knobs and dials and buttons make engaging with technology feel more deliberate. And for that reason, despite its faults, the Duo won me over. It’s a phone that’s compelling and fun — a phone I enjoyed picking up, but also found easier to put down.
