The One Real Danger of Donald Trump’s Mary Peltola Threats
2026-10-31

The One Real Danger of Donald Trump’s Mary Peltola Threats

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Election mayhem season is upon us and it’s best to be prepared. In just the past week, Trump officials have sued to potentially block the certification of a Senate race in Alaska. They’ve threatened local California election officials with jail time. They have certainly implied that the outcome in a Senate race in Minnesota can’t be trusted unless the Republican wins. On this week’s Amicus podcast, Mother Jones’ national voting rights correspondent, Ari Berman, joined Dahlia to explain what we already know in terms of the attempts to throw sand in the gears of voting, and how all that is informing him about what to look out for this coming week. Their conversation, edited and condensed for clarity, follows. Dahlia Lithwick: When this Alaska lawsuit first hit, it did not pass the laugh test, right? Ari Berman: I mean, it was straight out of the Four Seasons Total Landscaping form of election law, in terms of how ridiculous it was. When I first started covering voting rights, the efforts by Republicans were to make it harder to vote before the election. Then what Trump did in 2020 was, he tried to throw out results after the election. Now, they’re trying to throw out results before the election. When we say that’s unprecedented, it really is. I can’t recall a single lawsuit like this that basically says, “If the candidate we like loses, you can’t certify it.” That’s basically what it said. I mean, it talked about some legal principles, but it was basically just a political document that said, “There’s two Dan Sullivans, and voters are evidently too stupid to figure out which one is which, even though one is on the ballot clearly identified as incumbent senator.” So they’re now asking a federal court to set aside the election if the Dan Sullivan they like doesn’t win, which I think has no chance of actually succeeding. But of course, this lawsuit’s not aimed at succeeding. It’s aimed at laying the groundwork to challenge the election after the fact. So now we really do have to talk about the skullduggery on the Democrat side, because we got a New York Times story this week about that other Dan Sullivan and how this all came to be. I want to give my cautions, because there is some context to be had. One, this ballot-stacking game is hardly new in American politics. And maybe more importantly, while it’s kind of gross, it’s certainly legal, and as you said, the ballot clearly names the incumbent. Nevertheless, skullduggery is skullduggery. Do you want to tell us what happened this week and whether and why it matters? The New York Times published a big piece. I would argue it was a slightly overheated and overhyped piece, in terms of how they presented it. The Times basically reported that the campaign, or operatives affiliated with the campaign, of Mary Peltola essentially recruited the other Dan Sullivan into the race to siphon votes away from Dan Sullivan, the incumbent senator of Alaska. To me, that seems like a political problem, not a legal problem. I don’t see how a court would change how they view this, because the Alaska Supreme Court already dealt with this issue. That’s the most important thing to know about this whole Alaska situation. This was already dealt with under state law by the Alaska Supreme Court. Officials in Alaska already tried to remove the other Dan Sullivan from the ballot, and the Alaska Supreme Court overruled them. So I can’t see how anything will change. Trump is basically saying on Truth Social that Peltola should not be able to run, not just that the other Dan Sullivan shouldn’t be able to run, but Peltola herself shouldn’t be able to run. The thing is, as you know, Dahlia, you can’t disqualify candidates via Truth Social. There’s a process here, and this process has already played out. To me, this feels like a political problem for Republicans in Alaska, and not a legal problem, even though the governor is saying they’re going to investigate, I don’t know what’s going to come of that. I do find it a little ironic that the Republican Party—which encouraged the hacking of a presidential candidate’s email, stole a Supreme Court seat, gerrymandered mid-decade, has done all sorts of things from Roger Stone to Lee Atwater to what Trump has done in terms of weaponizing the entire federal government, trying to overturn the 2020 election—is now saying this is election interference. They’re not exactly coming from, like, the high moral ground here. Right. It is that asymmetry that’s always a fork in our eye. When we go low, we freak out, and when they go low, we are just like, “Oh, well, that’s what they do.” So the asymmetry never fails to trip us up. And now the president has not just called for the Democratic nominee to be disqualified, but also criminally prosecuted, because he never wastes an opportunity to waste our time. Apparently in his view, this is a crime by the nominee. It just goes back to “lock her up.” It goes back to how he’s using his Justice Department as basically the revenge part of his government. They’re going after James Comey. They’re going after whoever he doesn’t like. Cassidy Hutchinson, even though they failed to actually indict her. I think it’s a lot of threats. I’m not concerned about that. What I am concerned about is this affecting election certification in Alaska. I think that’s the thing we should pay attention to. Because of ranked choice voting in Alaska, they don’t even tabulate the results of ranked choice voting until 14 days after the election, meaning it’s going to take a while to count the votes in Alaska. There’s going to be a ton of disinformation during that time, particularly if the Alaska Senate race is the seat that determines control of the U.S. Senate, which is entirely possible given how close it is. I think you’re going to see a lot of pressure not to certify the election brought to bear on Republican officials in Alaska. That’s where they’re going to rerun the 2020 playbook. Even though courts have very clearly said election certification is ministerial, not optional. That is one of the really big things we now know from 2020. You can’t just not certify an election because you don’t like who won the election. That said, they’re going to lean very heavily to not certify the election, and that’s why I would be more concerned, for example, about a situation like Alaska than California. Because in California, they have no power whatsoever to force the state to do anything. In Alaska, they could at least lean on the governor and the lieutenant governor to try not to certify. Then it would go to court; it might very well go back to the Supreme Court. I would imagine the election would end up being certified, but there is some uncertainty here in terms of how the process could break down, and obviously, the litigation by the Justice Department and then, more to the point, all the things the president has done to increase the chance that they’ll try to challenge the results of that election Just because they haven’t succeeded, and they’re deplorably bad at it, does that mean we shouldn’t worry, or doesn’t it matter how bad you are at it. If you want to unsettle election results, even doing it badly can have catastrophic results? It would be incredibly naive not to worry, but I think it’s worth noting that they incited an insurrection, but they didn’t succeed in overturning the election, which was their ultimate goal. And it’s a lot harder to overturn a midterm than a presidential election because there are so many different elections. It’s so decentralized. We’re not talking about five or six swing states here. We’re talking about House and Senate and governor’s races all across the country. Now, could it come down to one Senate race in a place like Alaska where they can try to manufacture some kind of dispute? Absolutely it could. But in terms of some of the hypotheticals—like not seating Democrats in the House, I mean, that would be very, very difficult to do. Just procedurally, it’s hard to do. It’s also hard to imagine it being within one or two seats. Nobody knows, but Democrats could win 25–35 seats in the House. It’s unlikely they’re going to win by one seat, based on how the polling is looking. And not just the polling, but look at where Trump is campaigning, where they’re spending money, all of those things. So the closer the election is, the better chance they have of contesting it. But I think the good news is, after 2020, there’s a lot we know about the system, and as I said earlier, certification is mandatory, not optional. The Justice Department can’t block certification of the election. They would have to convince someone not to do so. I think it’s very unlikely they will be able to convince a court not to do so, because there’s so much settled law from 2020 and after that you have to certify the election results. Absent a legitimate dispute about the election, a Bush v. Gore or a Franken versus Norm Coleman type scenario, we’re talking about a few hundred votes that are reasonably disputed. Absent that, the fact that you don’t like a candidate’s name, for example, that’s not going to lead to a lack of certification. In terms of leaning on local officials, that’s where I would be most concerned. Where do the vulnerabilities lie? There are new Trump-aligned people in power at the county level, and at the state level. Election deniers have taken over some of these positions. So that’s where I would be concerned that someone at the local or county level would try to block certification, and then it would have to go back to the courts, and the courts would ultimately say, “You have to certify the election.” I’m pretty confident of that. But again, that’s where the chaos and the uncertainty comes into the process. So the Alaska thing to me was a signal that they’re not going to be legally successful in overturning the election, but they’re going to try. There was a question before of whether they would even try. I guessed they would, but we hadn’t seen the evidence of that until now. Harmeet Dhillon was on 60 Minutes saying they’d only challenge it in crazy circumstances, and then they filed two lawsuits in the same week that are basically designed to block certification. So we now know they’re going for it, which shouldn’t be surprising. But going for it is different than winning. To me, this is more of a Hail Mary than it is like they’re on the goal line about to kick the game-winning field goal. So many people have done work behind the scenes now to prepare. A lot of people were caught off guard in 2020. They didn’t really think Trump was going to go through with it. They saw the hair dye falling down Rudy Giuliani’s face, and they just laughed it off and never thought it would get to an insurrection. Now people take this seriously, and the amount of work that’s been done behind the scenes and the amount of work that’s been done to Trump-proof the system and protect election officials makes me think they’re going to try some of this stuff, but it’s going to be very hard for them to actually pull it off, particularly in a midterm environment. Are people really going to storm the Capitol over a House race in Kentucky or a Senate race in Alaska? I mean, I guess it’s conceivable, but I just have a hard time seeing it happen. I think at the end of the day, rather than going all the way to Jan. 3, Trump is probably going to be really pissed off, blame a lot of different people, and go golfing. There’s a Likely Scenario for Election Night. It’s Scary for Democrats. It’s Scarier for Democracy.
2026-10-31

There’s a Likely Scenario for Election Night. It’s Scary for Democrats. It’s Scarier for Democracy.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this weekend’s edition of the Surge, a politics newsletter whose job is not to make predictions in battleground Senate races and refuses to do so. (Unless asked, just one time and without much pushing at all, in a live show like the one we hosted on Thursday.) In this last edition before the election, we don’t have any big, sweepy analysis to make on the State of the Elections—that’s coming in a separate piece we’ve written for Monday—but here’s the long and short of it: Polls are very good for Democrats. Polls could be wrong. Polls could be right? Instead, we look at a few of the more discrete developments in the past week. The Michigan Democratic Senate primary drama between Abdul El-Sayed and Haley Stevens, one of the summer’s most watched programs, is back with a Halloween special. And hey, Barack Obama? You can come and take these cheeseburger spring rolls from our cold, dead hands. One note of housekeeping: The Surge will be doing a sleep-deprived live show for Slate Plus subscribers at noon ET the day after the election, analyzing results and assigning blame for the things we got wrong to other people. Now let’s turn our attention to the ongoing foofaraw in Alaska. The New York Times this week published a story detailing what everyone has long expected but hadn’t proved: that Democratic operatives associated with the Mary Peltola campaign and in Washington were behind getting a second “Dan Sullivan” on the Alaska Senate ballot to sow confusion among voters. Both D.C. groups and the Peltola campaign denied involvement to the Times, but c’mon. No doubt this is a trick designed to confuse Sullivan voters and get some of them to select the wrong Sullivan on the ranked choice ballot, without listing Sen. Sullivan as their second choice. The Alaska Supreme Court did a decent job, though, of sorting this out. While it didn’t find reason to toss Decoy Sullivan off the ballot—at the end of the day, eligible candidates are allowed to run for office—it did get Sen. Sullivan to be listed as the Republican incumbent on the ballot. The Democrats behind this aren’t as clever as they think they are. If they didn’t want to be caught, they should have pursued a spoiler strategy that didn’t involve picking a random guy with the same name: It was an instant giveaway that this wasn’t on the level. And the modern compulsion to text cheeky little jokes alluding to the scheme, once again, claims new victims. In terms of the remedy for this, though, everyone needs to get a grip. What Democrats did was recruit a spoiler candidate. That’s not new, and it wasn’t the only such effort to recruit spoiler candidates in this very race this year. The system then worked, with Alaska courts clarifying the ballot, Sen. Sullivan using the spoiler as an argument against Peltola in the campaign, and the media uncovering what had happened. If Alaska voters are so disgusted by what Peltola and Democrats did, they should vote against her as punishment. There’s a straightforward political remedy here. But that’s not the direction this is headed. The Department of Justice, which had already sued to block certification of the race should Sen. Sullivan lose reelection, is now issuing a flurry of subpoenas. Alaska’s governor has launched his own probe looking into civil or criminal penalties and is refusing to say whether he would certify Peltola as the winner. House Oversight Committee Chairman James Comer has started an investigation too. Trump, of course, has called for Peltola to be prosecuted. And if Peltola is certified as the winner and sent to Washington, he could urge Senate Republicans to object to her swearing-in, setting up a genuine shitstorm that the Surge would prefer not to cover. We do not believe that the remedy for Democrats recruiting a spoiler candidate should be for the Senate to start exercising discretion on which election winners it chooses to seat. We hate to sound like Mr. Rogers here, but: Let’s hope for a clean winner, one way or another. Because the Trump administration’s preferred path in a muddled result isn’t democracy-enhancing. When we visited Michigan before the Democratic Senate primary this summer, then-candidate Haley Stevens unequivocally told us that despite the nastiness of the primary, she would “absolutely” support Abdul El-Sayed if he became the nominee. She graciously followed through on that, at first, after losing the primary in a closer-than-expected result. Since then, she’s enjoyed some time off, some TMZ appearances, interviews here and there. She’s planning a wedding. What she hadn’t really said much about until this week, though, was the Senate race. And now she’s saying an awful lot. In an appearance on a CNN panel last week, she would say only that she was “still on the team” with El-Sayed (without mentioning his name), while offering a bevy of gentle criticisms about his campaigning tactics and observing that GOP candidate Mike Rogers is outhustling him in Detroit. Later in the week, it came out that her fiancé was supporting Rogers. Then, Stevens did another primetime interview with CNN, where she mentioned that she still supports “Abdul” but again lined it with criticism of his ads and observed that they might be making voters feel unsafe. What’s going on here? We don’t know—some combination of still being uncomfortable with El-Sayed’s Israel–Palestine politics, spending a lot of time with a fiancé who loathes El-Sayed, and a bit of sour grapes from the primary. It does appear that South Carolina Rep. Jim Clyburn, who endorsed Stevens in the primary, has come to play peacemaker and get the two ex-(?)rivals to appear together over the weekend. Stevens has continued to press El-Sayed, though, to more fully denounce a left-wing super PAC ad that was criticized as antisemitic. Stevens’ interventions this week won’t soon be forgotten by progressives. The big question posed by El-Sayed’s candidacy was whether someone from the Bernie wing of the Democratic Party could win a statewide Senate race in a true purple state. His polling has been better this week. But for most of the cycle, he’s been underperforming more-moderate candidates in other battleground states and substantially underperforming Michigan’s Democratic gubernatorial candidate. Now, win or lose—but especially lose!—the progressive movement will blame the underperformance on Democratic establishment figures for undercutting him. And the first name out of their mouths will be Stevens’. At a rally last weekend, former President Barack Obama joked that Trump’s “got the White House looking like Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory.” Life has been incredible ever since. While this joke is not offensive to anyone, many feigned offense, with the interpretation being that Obama, ever the professorial elitist, was looking down on the good folks of Real America who enjoy the Cheesecake Factory. Stunts were pulled. The administration sent Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent—a near-billionaire who likely hasn’t dined at a chain restaurant since before he and George Soros shorted the British pound in the early ’90s—to pretend to enjoy eating lunch at the Cheesecake Factory. “I had a great meal, always have a great meal, and I would just tell the Martha’s Vineyard set to give it a try,” the finance tycoon told the press outside the restaurant. The White House X account released an A.I. slop video of Trump waving to the assembled masses outside a Cheesecake Factory. The Wall Street Journal editorial board published a column titled “Obama Goes Socialist, Scorns Cheesecake” (subhead: “He throws in with Florida’s Angie Nixon and condescends to Middle America”). For our younger Surge readers who may not have been politically aware during the Obama presidency: Every day was like this, for eight years. In retrospect: How charming. We’d analogize this to the moment in Trump’s 2024 campaign when a comedian joked, at a late-campaign rally, that Puerto Rico was “a floating island of garbage.” Now, that joke did actually offend plenty of people, while Obama’s joke offended zero people. But Democrats played it well beyond its worth as they tried to make something happen in the last week of a campaign they were going to lose. As you are all well aware, it did not prevent Democrats from losing that election. And—GUESS WE’RE GOING TO WRITE THE FOLLOWING WORDS—CheesecakeFactoryGate will not be the thing that prevents Democrats from doing well this year, if there is such a thing. • One key moment in Susan Collins’ 2020 reelection campaign was an endorsement ad featuring local broadcasting legend Bill Green. Green was a household name, and previously apolitical, so his endorsement carried weight. So we weren’t surprised to see Collins close with another, almost identically filmed ad featuring Green six years later. But the message Green delivers this time around is more pointed. In the ad, Green says that he knows Democratic nominee Troy Jackson, a logger by trade, but that “we’re not talking about buying a cord of wood here.” He adds that “Troy doesn’t have the tools he needs to be effective” in the U.S. Senate. That line allowed the Jackson campaign to respond that Collins’ team was condescending. Not as condescending as a joke involving the Cheesecake Factory, mind you. But nothing is. • If there’s a substantial polling error underestimating Republicans, this Washington Post write-up of its poll, which shows Democrat Adam Hamilton leading Sen. Roger Marshall in Kansas by 9 points, is a great explanation of why. Excited Democratic voters are sitting by their phones, waiting for pollsters to call, while pollsters are having a difficult time getting in touch with MAGA voters and divining a representative sample. The counter to that would be that with Trump off the ballot, maybe these voters aren’t answering polls because they’re less likely to vote, so the two effects net out to an extent. We’ll see. We’ll see!!! • We haven’t gotten a lot of public polls showing how Ohio gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy’s grotesque ad treating the wife of an alleged rapist as a reliable narrator is playing in the state. But according to outlets that have seen private GOP polling, it has hurt Ramaswamy pretty badly. Such a shame. Couldn’t have happened to a nicer guy! The ad is no longer running. • After the Supreme Court’s Callais decision, which gutted protections for predominantly minority congressional districts, Tennessee Republicans quickly nuked the Democratic district in Memphis. But the race to represent what remains of Tennessee’s 9th District is drawing some attention and outside support for the Republican candidate, Brent Taylor, from Trump’s super PAC. This week, the Nashville Scene reported that Taylor “has sent unsolicited nude photos as recently as 2021.” Taylor, while not specifically confirming or denying the allegations, alluded to “youthful indiscretions” from his past that he’s worked through with his wife. Here’s the thing about those “youthful indiscretions,” though: Brent Taylor is 58 years old, and 2021 was five years ago. • Not to be a downer (totally to be a downer), but if Republicans lose both chambers of Congress next week because they’re yoked to an incredibly unpopular president, how does he respond? How does he change the subject? The answer may be right there in front of us: a resumption of the war in Iran. We’ll see you next week, treasured reader. There will be much to discuss. The Trump administration has sued to potentially block certification of a Senate race in Alaska, threatened California election officials with jail time, and challenged the voter rolls in 30 states. Trump’s Iran War Strategy: Out of the Frying Pan, Into the Fire
2026-10-30

Trump’s Iran War Strategy: Out of the Frying Pan, Into the Fire

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. If Republicans get thumped in next week’s midterms, Donald Trump’s war in Iran will be a big reason why. Many voters see the 8-month-old, $47 billion–and–counting conflict as a glaring violation of the president’s campaign pledge not to start new wars—one that has sent gas prices spiraling higher to boot. Yet regardless of just how much the elections end up looking like a rejection of Trump’s military adventurism, there are mounting signs that the president plans to escalate the fighting shortly after polls close on Tuesday. Let’s read the tea leaves. They’re everywhere. As the Wall Street Journal reported this week, U.S. officials have told America’s allies in the Gulf to brace for “a short, intense military campaign” after the elections. Per the New York Times, the Pentagon has drawn up plans for new strikes targeting Iran’s energy infrastructure, missile batteries, drone arsenal, and nuclear facilities. The U.S. is shipping more weapons to the region and preemptively evacuating embassies and military bases that Iran could target for retaliation. More U.S. warships are en route, carrying thousands of Marines. Iran and the Gulf states caught in the crossfire, meanwhile, are frenziedly stocking up on missiles and interceptors as if anticipating a return to hostilities. The president has publicly pledged not to strike Iran before the midterms, but he has also hinted that he has plans to do so once voting wraps. This month, Trump suggested that the U.S. would win the war “right after the election” and said, “We may do another little Epic Fury,” referencing the code name for the joint U.S.-Israeli strikes that started it. It doesn’t seem very Trumplike to show restraint. Why is he waiting until after the midterms, anyway? Carl Von Clausewitz once famously argued (and I’m both paraphrasing and translating here, since the dude spoke German) that war is the continuation of politics by other means. As the midterms have neared, though, this particular war has been more like the inverse: Domestic politics are dictating Trump’s approach to the fighting. Instead of greenlighting new attacks on Tehran, Trump has tasked the U.S. Navy with helping oil tankers transit the embattled Strait of Hormuz in a bid to hold down gas prices and save GOP seats. Iran, meanwhile, has been doing everything it can to keep prices high, upping attacks on tankers and having its proxies target Saudi Arabian oil refineries. After the vote, though, those incentives could flip. Unconstrained by Republicans nervous about losing their seats, Trump might escalate the fighting to try to force Iran to capitulate. So could the next attack be worse than what’s come before? Some Democratic lawmakers seem to think so. Chris Murphy of Connecticut, a member of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee who has been privy to classified battle plans, recently described the administration’s postelection strategy as “insane.” Without giving details, Murphy hinted that Trump could target civilian infrastructure in ways that would “lead to devastating escalation and the likely destruction of our commitment to the rule of law.” Um, yikes. What could that mean for the conflict? It depends on who you believe. According to the Atlantic, some Trump confidantes think U.S. sanctions have sent Iran’s hardline leadership “close to the edge,” and that another round of attacks “would push them over.” Other analysts think an escalation will just embolden Iran, which has already endured thousands of U.S. strikes. Well, keep in mind that elections can have consequences. If Democrats retake Congress, they could muster enough votes to block funding for the war. If Israeli opposition parties put aside their differences after this week’s election and topple Benjamin Netanyahu as prime minister, Trump could lose his biggest partner in the fight. And if the war keeps dragging on, it could become a millstone for Vice President J.D. Vance—or whoever becomes the next Republican presidential nominee—in the next election, too. They don’t call them forever wars for nothing. Without minimizing the scariness of war, it’s time for a hard pivot. Spooky season culminates tomorrow, and my colleagues have put together a frightfully good list of what to brew, read, and dress up as to get your weekend off to the right start: Creepy cocktails: Double, double toil and trouble! Plenty of people are brewing themed cocktails to celebrate Halloween, but too often these drinks wind up as tricks instead of treats. Courtney Iseman breaks down why gimmicky cocktails go wrong—and gives some recipe ideas for making your spooky spirits a smashing success. Icelandic Dracula: Shortly after Bram Stoker’s Dracula was published, a translation of the novel appeared in Iceland, with some very noticeable differences from the original. Where did Nordic Dracula come from? Who wrote this version? And did Stoker really give it his blessing? Tune in to this spooky episode of Decoder Ring for “a seasonally appropriate literary mystery—one full of doppelgängers, supervillains, plot twists, and vampires who just refuse to die.” Spirit Halloween: Have you ever wondered what it’s like to work at the seasonal costume superstore Spirit Halloween? Luke Winkie spoke with a 20-year-old assistant manager at one, and it turns out that it’s as bloodcurdling as it seems. Giant skeletons: Home Depot’s 12-foot-tall skeletons are some of the most coveted—and noticeable—Halloween decorations around. They first went viral in 2020, and they have been a hit since, towering over yards across America in all kinds of inventive tableaux. Heather Schwedel spoke with a Home Depot merchant to get the inside scoop on how these giant skeletons came to be. Enjoy the weekend! News and Politics - Slate Magazine
The Truth About the Alaska Dan Sullivan Shenanigans
2026-10-30

The Truth About the Alaska Dan Sullivan Shenanigans

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Earlier this week, the New York Times published a splashy feature detailing how allies of Mary Peltola, the Democratic nominee in this year’s Senate race in Alaska, worked to “stack” the ballot with candidates who could help her defeat the Republican incumbent, Dan Sullivan. As a result of these efforts, when Alaskans go to the polls next week, appearing alongside Peltola and Sullivan on the ballot will be one Daniel J. Sullivan, a retired teacher who voted for Peltola in previous elections—and who, according to the Times, was recruited by Democratic-operative types to play the spoiler role. The Times’ implicit framing of Daniel J. Sullivan’s candidacy as an election-swinging dirty trick requires a few caveats. First and most important, Alaska uses ranked choice voting, which means that Sen. Sullivan supporters confused by their two options can simply vote for both. Second, Peltola beat Sen. Sullivan by 8 points in the August primary, and Daniel J. Sullivan picked up 2.5 percent of the vote—significant, sure, but nowhere close to Peltola’s margin of victory. Third, Alaska officials have taken great pains to distinguish the two Sullivans on the ballot, displaying their names differently and designating Dan Sullivan as the incumbent. Also, just speaking practically here, if you believed that the presence in a tightly contested Senate race of a person with the same name as a vulnerable incumbent in a tough year for Republicans was a complete coincidence, I have it on good authority that the bridge to nowhere is getting built after all, and I would sell it to you for a favorable price. None of these facts have prevented President Donald Trump and his fellow Republicans from getting very angry about this stunt. Before the Times story was published, Justice Department officials, at the president’s direction, had filed a lawsuit seeking to block state officials from certifying the results if Peltola beats Dan Sullivan by less than Daniel J. Sullivan’s share of the vote. (Again, thanks to ranked choice voting, this scenario is extremely farfetched, but stay with me.) After the Times story, Trump went much further, calling for criminal charges against Peltola and the “FAKE Dan Sullivan.” He also demanded that both of them be removed from the ballot, something that would give Republicans nervous about losing the Senate one fewer seat to worry about. Sure enough, on Wednesday, Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy, a Republican, announced that state officials were looking at “whether this scheme violated Alaska’s laws.” The DOJ, which had an eye on the race already, issued new subpoenas to Democrats linked to the non-senator Sullivan, whose candidacy Attorney General Todd Blanche described as “cheating” and “fraud.” As is true of all breathless invocations of voter fraud by Republican politicians, however, Blanche’s claims do not hold up to scrutiny. The reality he is glossing over is that, as you would probably expect, there have already been legal challenges to Daniel J. Sullivan’s candidacy, based in part on some of the details in the Times story. In June, though, an Alaska court determined that under state law, he has the same right to run for office as Sen. Sullivan or anyone else. What Peltola’s campaign (allegedly) did might be partisan skulduggery, but it is probably not illegal, and no one is getting kicked off the ballot or going to prison over it. This case started back in May, when Daniel J. Sullivan filed to run for Senate as a Republican, despite never having previously been affiliated with the party. After the state GOP complained, the Alaska Division of Elections determined that under state law, Daniel J. Sullivan was ineligible to run because he had not filed “in good faith for the purpose of genuinely pursuing election” and had instead sought to “confuse or mislead” voters. The letter noted, for example, his request to be identified as “Dan,” like the incumbent. It also alluded to the subject matter of the Times story, highlighting his work with a political consultant who is a “known longtime supporter of Democratic candidates,” including Peltola. Sullivan appealed, and in June, an Alaska trial court judge reversed the Division of Elections’ decision. The gist of Judge Thomas A. Matthews’ order, which the Alaska Supreme Court later affirmed, is that under state law, the qualifications to run for Senate straightforwardly track the language of the U.S. Constitution: Candidates must be at least 30 years old, have been citizens for at least nine years, and live in the state they seek to represent. Given that Daniel J. Sullivan checks all three boxes, Matthews found that officials had no authority to invent an additional “good faith” requirement and use it to “exclude an otherwise qualified candidate.” Matthews’ opinion does not suggest that he is naive about what’s going on here. But critical to his analysis is the fact that under state law, the Division of Elections could have previously taken steps to prevent this from happening but didn’t: He points out that other states have dealt with same-name races, and that many have rules for weighing the “good faith” of prospective candidates. Since Alaska officials “could have predicted this situation would eventually arise,” Matthews concluded, they “may not now assert a new requirement, standard, limitation, or qualification after presentation of the candidate’s declaration.” The attention generated by the Times story has Republicans looking to impose consequences that go well beyond the confines of Alaska election law. The challenge they face is that, as the Times acknowledged, ballot-stacking techniques are “common in politics,” are “generally considered legal,” and have been “employed by both parties in Alaska.” Peltola knows this better than most: In 2024, when she lost her bid for reelection to the House, Republicans went to court to ensure that Eric Hafner, who is serving a 20-year federal prison sentence in New York for threatening to kill judges, remained on the ballot as a Democrat. During the primary campaign in this very election, outside groups were pouring money into the state, hoping to sabotage Peltola by placing other Democrats on the general election ballot. Intuitively, recruiting a candidate with the same name as another candidate feels like a more nefarious form of ballot stacking. But so far, Republicans’ efforts to draw a legal distinction have been vague, speculative, nonsensical, or some combination thereof. On Truth Social, for example, Trump claimed that the Times had revealed that Democrats had lied “under oath in Federal Court,” which is an accurate description of a crime, but not a thing that happened here. The Wall Street Journal reported that federal prosecutors are “exploring” whether to “bring charges alleging a conspiracy to violate voters’ rights.” There are real laws that protect these rights, but prosecutors generally use them to target people who threaten voters or try to trick them into not voting—for example, this guy, who in 2016 posted memes urging Hillary Clinton supporters to “vote” via text message. (Even that conviction was overturned on appeal.) Whatever you think of Daniel J. Sullivan’s candidacy, it is not an illegal effort to stop people from voting: He is running for office, under his real name, in the state where he lives. Anyone who gets “tricked” into voting for him (and only for him) is still, in fact, voting. Even if one of the more ambitious Trump prosecutors decides to give it a shot, the First Amendment will present serious challenges, since going after candidates and their supporters for ostensibly harboring ulterior motives would criminalize a lot of things that parties in this country already do. In context, the threats Republicans are issuing right now are best understood not as fully baked legal theories but as performances intended to convey to Trump that they, too, are angry about getting played and are looking into it very strongly. Dunleavy, Alaska’s governor, told the Wall Street Journal that he informed Trump that he is “investigating at the state level,” which is what I would tell the incensed leader of my party if I knew he wanted me to do something but there was little I could do and less than a week in which to do it. The more likely (and more serious) implication of the story is the possibility of Trump and his allies using it as the basis of a new round of “stolen election” allegations. By loudly investigating the matter now, they are leaving open the possibility of challenging the results, not with oafish phone calls or a horde of Trump supporters storming the Capitol, but with the institutional power of the Justice Department. Citing unproven allegations to pressure Alaska officials not to certify a hypothetical Peltola victory—Dunleavy has said he is open to holding up the process, “depending upon the outcomes of this investigation”—feels like a long shot, but if control of the Senate comes down to Alaska, Trump is at least going to take it. The one bit of good news here is that if this Alaska Public Media report is any indication, actual voters do not care all that much. One described the allegations as “nothing that we haven’t heard before.” Others expressed frustration with all the media focus on the ballot, rather than the policy positions of the people on it. Still others understandably took offense at the notion that Alaskans are not smart enough to figure out how to vote. This is shady business. But for better and for worse, in this country, “shady” is just how politics works. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
This Week’s Election Revealed a Troubling Truth About Israeli Politics
2026-10-30

This Week’s Election Revealed a Troubling Truth About Israeli Politics

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Israel’s election this week was so close that neither of the contending coalitions—Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s or the opposition, led by former military chief Gadi Eisenkot—may emerge with the majority needed to govern. The question now is this: Does it matter which side ultimately wins? It depends on the subject. When it comes to whether Israel resumes its war against Iran, the election’s outcome will make some difference. As for curbing the violence of Jewish settlers in the West Bank, it might have a little bit of impact. On the war in Gaza, there is no daylight between the coalitions. And on the prospect of settling the broad conflict between Jews and Palestinians, the election uncloaked an aspect of Israeli politics that will likely worsen tensions, regardless of the final vote count. The key factor is this. Hamas’ murderous raid of Oct. 7, 2023; Israel’s wildly excessive retaliation; and the inability of outside parties to impose an arrangement that ends the war, empowers a peaceful Palestinian Authority, and repairs the devastation in Gaza—all of these acts of ultraviolence and neglect have frozen Israeli politics in a state of seemingly permanent trauma. Hamas will not disarm until Israel withdraws from Gaza; Israel will not withdraw until Hamas disarms. Both positions are, in their way, understandable. Meanwhile, the vast majority of Israelis—even those who were once lefties or pacifists—consider it suicidal to so much as think about surrendering territory to Palestinians, much less negotiating a separate Palestinian state. One surprising outcome of the election made this fact all too clear. Israel’s Arab parties won 19 parliamentary seats—almost twice as many as the 10 they won in the prior election. If either of the two main Jewish candidates—Netanyahu or Eisenkot—invited the Arabs to join his coalition, he would gain more than enough seats to win a majority and thus lead the government. (Netanyahu’s bloc won 53 votes, and Eisenkot’s 48; it takes 61—half of the Knesset’s 120 seats, plus 1—to form a majority.) However, neither politician is likely to do that. Netanyahu has no desire to court the favor of Arabs, and even if he did for the sake of retaining power, he would probably lose as many far-right partners from his coalition as he would gain. Eisenkot, while not quite as extreme on the matter, promised before the election not to form coalitions with parties that don’t formally declare Israel to be a Jewish state—a stance that was widely (and accurately) taken to mean an oath not to partner with Arab parties. The Arab parties do not represent the roughly 5 million Palestinians in Gaza or the West Bank; those residents of occupied territories lack the right to vote in Israeli elections at all. Rather, they were supported by the 2.1 million Arabs who live in Israel proper—and almost all of them are Sunni Muslims, Christian Arabs, or Druze. Over the years, Israelis have fended off charges that they live in an apartheid state by pointing to the equal rights held by these Arabs. Yet, in a season when the Arab parties have won enough seats to make them potential kingmakers, the Jewish parties—their leaders, and to a great extent their constituents—have decided, at least for now, that they’d rather forgo certain victory if it meant giving Arabs positions in the government. This blatant denial of political power to Israeli Arabs, even when they’ve earned it through peaceful democratic means, would further isolate Israel from its allies—and could radicalize Israeli Arabs who until now were content with working within their country’s political system. The double standard has always been there, but now it’s too obvious to pretend away or disguise. The question of whether the election will make a difference when it comes to Jewish settlers’ violence against Palestinians in the West Bank is more complicated. Eisenkot has spoken out against the government’s tolerance—in many instances blithe encouragement—of the violence. If Netanyahu is thrown out of power, so will his main henchmen on this issue, Itamar Ben-Gvir and Bezalel Smotrich, the minister of national security and the finance minister, respectively, who believe that Gaza and the West Bank should be formally annexed. However, while Eisenkot has opposed annexation, he is in favor of continued—even expanded—occupation. And to the extent the occupation is a root cause of the tensions and violence in the area, its problems will persist. On other matters, Netanyahu and his Jewish opponents differ more distinctly. Netanyahu—who has been prime minister, on and off, for 19 years, longer than any other in Israel’s 78 years as a nation-state—has clearly stated that he wants to resume full-scale war on Iran for the purpose of changing its regime. Eisenkot has dismissed regime change as a fantasy and has said he’d prefer to sway Tehran’s behavior—away from supporting terrorist groups and developing nuclear weapons—through economic pressure. Still, Netanyahu remains prime minister until the election is settled, and if he decides to start bombing Iran again, and if Iran bombs Israel in return, Eisenkot—the former top general of Israel’s military—would not likely oppose further retaliation against Iran. Some speculate that under those circumstances, he might accept calls to form a “unity government,” joining the two coalitions. It would be a tenuous, probably short-lived government, since the two leaders—and their followers—hate each other, but it would presumably be a popular move for the short term. However, this is theoretical speculation, as Netanyahu is unlikely to resume bombing unless he gets at least the green light, if not active cooperation, from Donald Trump. The American president has said he might start bombing again after next week’s midterm elections—a telling qualifier. He wouldn’t do so before the midterms because he knows that the war is unpopular and that its resumption could wreck the Republicans’ already-slim chance of hanging on to the House—and possibly to the Senate as well. No one knows what Trump may do after the midterms; perhaps Trump himself doesn’t know. If the Republicans lose, especially if they lose big, will he take that as a sign to steer away from Middle East wars, as he long ago promised voters he would do? Will a Democratic Congress vote to forbid him from resuming attacks on Iran (as the narrowly Republican-controlled Congress almost did)? Will Trump abide by that vote or ignore it as a violation of his Article 2 powers? Trump endorsed Netanyahu in these elections and, as a result, will no doubt take credit—perhaps legitimately—for the prime minister’s Likud party winning a larger share of votes than the polls, and even exit polls, had predicted (though far fewer than it won in the previous election). However, what happens next is beyond Trump’s control. If neither faction can form a majority, then new elections would have to be called. In the interim, Netanyahu would remain prime minister, but his supporters would form only a minority of parliament. This gridlock could persist for months. The stalemate would hobble the state, which might compel Netanyahu and his opponents to make some kind of deal, either with each other or with the Arab parties. Or maybe they won’t be able to pull off such a feat. The divisions in Israeli politics and society may be too severe to mask—and any deal could aggravate tensions among the coalitions’ constituents. The region’s wars have hit a lull in recent weeks, but they are far from over. The dynamics unleashed by the elections—Israel’s and ours—could extend the calm or recharge the violence. Google's AI satellite sends a very expensive haiku to Earth
2026-10-31

Google's AI satellite sends a very expensive haiku to Earth

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry Google on Friday celebrated receiving a poem from its first experimental orbital AI datacenter satellite. The company's Suncatcher satellite, launched at the start of the month and put into low Earth orbit, has responded to a ground station prompt to "Write a haiku from the perspective of a TPU orbiting Earth, with the wonderful world spinning beneath you." Travis Beals, senior director of Google's interdisciplinary Paradigms of Intelligence research team, said that with Suncatcher's host and TPU trays powered up and machine learning tests completed, the onboard Gemma 4 model achieved its "Hello World" milestone by sending an inference response from orbit. "During a ground-station pass, we uplinked our first prompt to our onboard Gemma 4 open model, asking it to generate a haiku about being in space," wrote Beals in a blog post. And as the satellite passed over Svalbard, Norway, the orbiting AI model responded over the downlink: Those roughly 20 tokens would cost about $0.000012 if generated through the Google Cloud API, based on Gemma 4 26B's pricing of $0.15 per million input tokens and $0.60 per million output tokens, excluding input costs. Add in a satellite weighing perhaps 500 kg, based on the mass of SpaceX's Starlink satellites, and a retail launch price of around $7,000 per kg, and you have a very expensive haiku. According to Google's calculations, if the cost of getting a satellite into low Earth orbit can be brought down to $200/kg, the launch cost amortized over the satellite's useful life (~$810–7,500/kW/year) could be comparable to current electricity spending for terrestrial datacenters in the US (~$570–3,000/kW/year). Google predicts that SpaceX could achieve that by 2035, more or less, and considers it strategically beneficial to prototype well in advance of that actually happening. After the haiku, Beals said, "we tested space-themed limericks and telemetry prompts reporting the technical status of the payload systems." Next up: tests for evaluating how TPUs perform under thermal stress and radiation. Google has already done some laboratory testing of its Trillium Cloud TPU and AMD host server to see how well they can withstand a 67 MeV proton beam. The system's high-bandwidth memory shows the most sensitivity in terms of the total ionizing dose – it exhibits irregularities after a cumulative dose of 2 krad(Si), which is about 3× Google's minimum requirement for TID survival of 750 rad(Si). "For typical transformer workloads, the observed SDC [silent data corruption] rate of approximately one event/17 rad, at an estimated orbital dose of 150 rad(Si)/year, translates to a very low failure probability on the order of 1 per 3 million inferences, assuming 1 inference per second," Google researchers said in a paper published earlier this month. "While this error rate is likely acceptable for inference, the impact of SEEs on training jobs, and the efficacy of system-level mitigations, requires further study." Indeed, the study will continue. Next year, the plan is to test a pair of interconnected satellites. Rather than lofting large satellites into orbit, Google anticipates sending a constellation of many smaller satellites that communicate with one another. ® Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Goth aesthetic gone wild: a user's self-inflicted black screen of non-death in Windows 3.1’s
2026-10-31

Goth aesthetic gone wild: a user's self-inflicted black screen of non-death in Windows 3.1’s

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Microsoft senior software engineer Raymond Chen has explained what Windows is doing when you hold down the power button, and shared a story about the time a customer accidentally created a Goth Mode for Windows 3.1. There's a section in The Hitchhiker's Guide to the Galaxy (actually, page 116 of this writer's dog-eared copy of The Restaurant at the End of the Universe) in which Zaphod Beeblebrox observes of a ship's interior: "Every time you operate one of these weird black controls that are labeled in black on black background, a little black light lights up black to let you know you've done it." The same turned out to be true of a user who called Microsoft customer support to complain that after Windows 3.1 booted up, all that remained after the product logo disappeared was a blank screen. It turned out the customer liked black a little too much and, according to Chen, made themself a Goth theme. The customer support engineer on Microsoft's end pieced it together. The customer had, according to Chen, "set the wallpaper color to black. They set the title bar color to black. They set the button color to black. They set the window background color to black. They set the window text color to black. "Every possible color you could pick in the Colors control panel was set to black." We suspect the mouse pointer, at least, would have stuck around, unless the customer had also managed to change some settings around this. And then there are the icons… However, Windows 3.1 was a long time ago, and this writer's attempts at theming then were the consequences of growing up in the '70s and '80s, which resulted in some interesting neon, orange, and brown combinations. As for Goth mode, Chen said, "Once you get yourself into this situation, it’s hard to get out because you can't see anything." One solution he suggested was deleting the [colors] section from the win.ini file. Chen told the anecdote as a coda to the question – what exactly is modern Windows doing when you hold down the power button on a PC? According to Chen, after eight seconds of holding the power button, the Windows kernel realizes a forced power-off is happening and tries to take a kernel dump for diagnostics. He explained that the kernel dumps were classified by Microsoft as "long power button hold" (LPBH) failures, with one of the bits of information captured indicating if all the pixels on the screen were black. The video driver might therefore be the culprit. Or whatever was responsible for drawing to the screen. Today's Windows struggles with dark mode. But in the dim, distant past, every pixel being black might mean the user was either Hotblack Desiato or had done something very silly indeed. ® Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Amazon sends chills through its workforce with mixed message memo about AI use
2026-10-31

Amazon sends chills through its workforce with mixed message memo about AI use

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
A lot of software engineers feel bleak about the future of the profession There's a ridiculously simple test you can use to figure out whether AI mandates at a given company are well-considered, or are being shoved out the door by out-of-touch buffoons: What happens when an engineer documents that AI made a task worse? Are they taken seriously, or are they marginalized and eventually managed out? Conveniently, this week Amazon published a treatise by Terk Terkowitz, a VP/Distinguished Engineer/Final Boss of Their Independent Contributor Technical Career Track, on the role of the developer in the age of AI. It was originally an internal missive sent to their software engineers and their managers back in September, which is an interesting lag for a document that sounds this confident. It also maps exceedingly well to what other organizations are struggling with. I think it's a good document, but I can't help but view it with a somewhat cynical eye. I see it as an attempt to make the future of the profession of software engineering feel less bleak. And it's feeling mighty bleak for a lot of folks right now. This treatise comes at a time of dramatic upheaval. Tech as an industry is no stranger to change (usually at 3AM, heralded by the pager), but something about the current AI-ification of the world strikes many of us as fundamentally dissimilar from previous revolutions. Folks are scared about their career prospects. "Should I give up tech and get into farming?" is a sincere question multiple people have asked me. I don't know! Tech is already a goat rodeo, so you've got a leg up if you do?This anxiety isn't theoretical. In May, Amazon wisely shut down KiroRank, an employee-built cash incineration leaderboard that ranked people by token usage, after the obvious thing happened: people spun up junk tasks to climb the ranks. This is now called "tokenmaxxing," and the Terkowitz treatise specifically calls it out as being at odds with "frugality." Meta did the same thing with "Claudenomics" and 85K employees that same week. And Amazon reportedly announced another 250 job cuts this week. I can't tell you that these things are related. But I can tell you that every engineer who read the memo assumes that they are. This document contains a lot of great stuff. I have a tremendous level of respect, both personal and professional, for Amazon's Principal Engineering community; I'm led to understand that that respect may not be reciprocated, but that's okay. I am, by their standards, a lapsed engineer who ran away to join the circus as one of its lesser clowns. Terkowitz's memo emphasizes customer outcomes and engineering judgement, two things I would absolutely not delegate to AI any time soon. (Note: I am writing this in October of 2026, with the full understanding that previous comments on the capabilities where AI falls short tend to age like fine milk.) It documents work that (today) AI tools aren't improving. That's way better than assuming that every objection to AI inherently comes from some self-interested Luddite who's scared of being replaced. It also says something smart about measurement: look at the 90th percentile of AI adoption, at those teams burning the most tokens, and check whether their delivery actually went up commensurately. This is great! Shout it from the rooftops; we should all be doing such things. The memo says “If your work does not benefit from current AI tooling, that is a finding, not a failing.” Document it, bring it to your manager, work through the problem. I agree with every word of that! The question is whether anyone's promo doc is gonna survive citing it. Here's what happens next. The memo concedes that input metrics are "deeply flawed," but then in the next paragraph says to go ahead and use them anyway, carefully. It then warns that non-adopters are falling behind. Those are three different positions within three sentences and they're not at all aligned with one another. That's not muddled; I think it's cover. A manager can cite "a finding, not a failing" when a strong engineer skips the AI tool, and then cite "falling behind" when it's time to PIP someone. Both are congruent with their stated policy; you get to decide which interpretation you want to go with in a given situation. How will managers handle justified AI non-use? Are promotion and performance decisions being made in a way that rewards these long term outcomes? Or are they instead taking the easy weasel way out, optimizing for what's cheap to measure, and rewarding tool activity? Because if you give me a metric, I will turn it into a target. So will an overwhelming majority of employees. When that happens, everyone loses — it's how KiroRank happened in the first place. I write this after two days of attending GitHub Universe, where they talked about how agents start roughly 40% of PRs now. And yet, Jay Parikh’s keynote argument was that useful output wasn't "more code," but rather completed outcomes: features shipped, bugs fixed, security issues addressed, loudmouth columnists silenced, etc. The problem here is that their entire business model cuts against that; they're billing in a model that charges customers on a consumption basis, not an output basis. There's nothing, today, that ties "AI spend" to "output that was worth the effort." Which brings us back to Amazon. The memo says tokenmaxxing is at odds with frugality, and for Amazon’s internal cost line, it is. Meanwhile AWS bills Bedrock and Kiro customers by the token. Inside the building, tokenmaxxing is a problem to be stamped out. Outside it, it’s the revenue plan. ® Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Chocolate Caterpillar or Chocolate Teapot? Top up the disk space for Windows 10 to find out

Visit Original Website
London's Oxford Street signage shows its underwear to passing tourists Today's bit of signage silage is either Windows 10 protesting that it is not part of London's Oxford Street pedestrianization, or someone failing to heed an alert before the public is made all too aware that things aren't going well… Eagle-eyed Register reader Aaron Colwill spotted the sign outside the food hall entrance of a Marks & Spencer store on London's Oxford Street. It usually entices passersby through the doors with promises of treats inside, but today it appears to require a different kind of feeding. Disk space is running low. Marks and Spencer (also known as M&S and Marks and Sparks) is no stranger to cyber whoopsies, having been the victim of an attack in 2025 in which crooks made off with customer data. Later that year, it reckoned cleanup costs would be about £136 million. That's quite a lot, but not enough to buy a bigger hard disk for Windows 10, it seems. Storage prices have increased over the years, but surely not that much? Colwill reckoned that Windows 10 21H2 LTSC was behind the problems. It could well be. While support for 21H2 ended for many users in 2023, Windows 10 LTSC 2021 will carry on until Jan 12, 2027, meaning engineers might have an exciting festive period ahead of them. Windows 10 IoT Enterprise LTSC 2021 will receive extended support until Jan 13, 2032. Either way, using Windows to advertise the Food Hall's wares seems slightly overkill. Simple posters, for example, never run out of disk space or attract the bork fairy's attention. Perhaps they might use something that lacks the voracious appetite for storage that Microsoft's finest demands. According to the Windows biz, installing Windows 10 requires at least 32 GB of disk space, ideally more. It all seems a bit excessive for a cake-based caterpillar advert. Backbone of a beast – charting Nvidia's rise to networking titan
2026-10-31

Backbone of a beast – charting Nvidia's rise to networking titan

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
How a $6.9B bet on Mellanox made Nvidia bigger than Cisco, and the world's most valuable company to boot Nvidia is more than just a leader in graphics and AI hardware. It has also become a titan of the networking industry with bigger datacenter Ethernet sales than any other vendor. In Q2, Nvidia's networking revenues topped $17.5 billion. To put that in perspective, Cisco's revenue during that same period, across all products, was $17.3 billion. GPUzilla's arrival as a networking power feels sudden, yet Jensen Huang started working towards this status in 2019 with the $6.9 billion acquisition of long-time networking partner Mellanox . At the time, Mellanox wasn't a big name in Ethernet switching. The company was mostly known for its ConnectX NICs and InfiniBand switches. Outside of high-performance computing (HPC) and supercomputing clusters, and a big deployment at Microsoft, InfiniBand wasn't widely deployed in hyperscale datacenters. Buying Mellanox allowed Nvidia to reposition itself as a system builder, which means something different in the context of distributed compute. Nvidia understood its datacenter customers weren't interested in buying GPUs or even servers; they wanted clusters of systems. For that, Nvidia needed interconnect and Mellanox's InfiniBand fit the bill. Over the years, Huang has talked about co-design – designing components and software to work together for specific set of jobs rather than to be the best GPU, switch, or NIC. Its work with Mellanox is a prime example of that approach. According to Dell'Oro Group analyst Sameh Boujelbene, Nvidia's decision to integrate compute and networking became key to the company's success. "Customers choose Nvidia for its tightly integrated compute and networking architecture, optimized for AI workloads," she told The Register. That integration makes deployments easier and speeds time to market. Ben Mabey, CTO of pioneering AI and machine learning company Recursion, told The Reg Nvidia's integration schemes won him over. Founded in 2013, nearly a decade before ChatGPT made AI the subject of dinner-table discussion, Recursion sought to apply machine learning running atop GPUs to the problem of drug discovery. This work involved churning through massive datasets which, Mabey tells us, now total more than 50 petabytes in size. "When you think about our workloads, it makes LLMs workload training seem easy compared to the amount of data that has to be moved around," he told The Register. "In the biological space and sciences in general, it's not well appreciated just how big the data actually is," he added. "The internet's big, but in the grand scheme of things, the text that people are training on is actually pretty small, versus the petabytes of data that we have of these images." Recursion's first cluster was a system called BioHive Zero which it cobbled together from GTX 1080 graphics cards originally intended for gaming systems. It used Ethernet to connect GPUs and ran into the limits of conventional networking. So, for its second cluster, an A100-based machine with around 300 accelerators, Recursion went with Nvidia's Quantum InfiniBand switches and ConnectX NICs. As Mabey explains it, buying its compute and interconnects from the same vendor vastly simplified optimization and troubleshooting. When Recursion reached out to Nvidia for help tuning the system, they were able to consider it as a single system, rather than discrete compute and networking resources. This level of integration had only previously been offered by boutique supercomputer builders like Cray or Atos (Eviden/Bull/whatever it gets renamed next). Nvidia hasn't always listed networking revenue as a single line item in its financials. However, based on CFO commentary and a little back of the napkin math we can fill in the blanks. Nvidia's networking revenues have grown steadily since the Mellanox acquisition. At two moments, its revenue shot up suddenly. The first came in the 2023 calendar year (FY24) after OpenAI kicked off the AI gold rush with the launch of ChatGPT. The second spread across 2024 and 2025, when Nvidia started selling Blackwell-generation GPUs and then started to ship them in large quantities. "I see two major inflection points, both coinciding with the Blackwell platform ramp: the introduction of NVLink switches and the accelerating adoption of Spectrum-X Ethernet switches for AI back-end networks, driven by growing deployments of Nvidia GPU clusters and increased attach rates of Nvidia switches, NICs, optics, and cables," Boujelbene said. It was around this time that users started deploy to Ethernet switches in large volume across Nvidia clusters. Elon Musk's Colossus supercomputer was one of the earliest examples. As we reported at the time, the first half of the 200,000-chip H100 and H200-based cluster came online in 2024 and, at the time, was among the largest ever clusters deployed using Nvidia's Spectrum-X Ethernet switches. Meta has also made extensive use of Nvidia's Spectrum switches in its own training clusters, including at its million-GPU Prometheus site in New Albany, Ohio. There are a couple reasons Nvidia's Spectrum-X offering is doing so well. First, through a combination of Spectrum Ethernet switches, superNICs, and some clever software optimizations, Nvidia was able to replicate many of the qualities that made InfiniBand so attractive while also benefiting from significantly higher radix and interoperability. Ethernet switches, including Nvidia's own, have historically offered more ports. The higher the radix, the fewer switches are necessary to support a cluster at a given port speed. This often gets misconstrued to mean that InfiniBand can't scale beyond clusters of a certain size, which isn't necessarily the case. But for clusters exceeding a certain number of GPUs, Ethernet is often more economical because it requires less equipment while achieving similar utilization. That said, Nvidia's Spectrum-X isn't exactly unique. Broadcom, Cisco, AMD and others have managed to achieve utilization similar to InfiniBand through various investments in smartNICs and switches. Nvidia's main differentiator, as Mabey and Boujelbene noted earlier, is the level of integration between the compute and networking. Then Blackwell hit the market, enabling much greater scale and creating a surge in Nvidia's networking revenue. Prior to Blackwell, Nvidia's NVLink and NVSwitch interconnect tech had largely been limited to single systems with up to eight GPUs. With the launch of the GB200 NVL72, these NVSwitches were broken out into dedicated appliances. (And yes, we remember GH200 NVL256 and NVL32, but from what we can tell these were never shipped in any meaningful volume, if at all.) For every NVL72 rack system sold, Nvidia also sold nine NVSwitch trays, which enabled the system's six dozen GPUs to function as a single enormous GPU. This effectively guaranteed steady networking revenue growth: even if customers didn't buy Nvidia's scale-out switches, if they wanted NVL72 racks, they were automatically buying NVSwitch. "We estimate that NVSwitch now accounts for roughly one-third of Nvidia’s networking revenue, up from virtually zero less than two years ago," Boujelbene told El Reg. "This underscores how quickly NVLink switching has become a major growth engine for Nvidia’s networking business." Nvidia is now moving to grow its networking business even further with a new licensing program. Until recently, Nvidia's NVSwitch tech was limited to its own accelerators. However, with the introduction of NVLink Fusion early last year, Nvidia opened the interconnect tech up to third parties to license. Many chip designers, including Marvell, Qualcomm, Arm, Fujitsu, MediaTek, and d-Matrix, have announced plans to develop CPUs and XPUs using NVLink Fusion. The adoption of the interconnect opens the door to additional NVSwitch sales, as the chips using NVLink Fusion still rely on them for chip-to-chip communications, and there's no other place to get them, as Nvidia has yet to license out NVSwitch. "NVLink Fusion allows Nvidia to extend its networking footprint beyond its own GPUs into the rapidly growing custom accelerator market. I see it as a strategic move to capture a larger share of AI networking spending, even in clusters that do not use Nvidia GPUs," Boujelbene said. Nvidia's networking ambitions don't stop there. "Nvidia continues to pursue opportunities in emerging networking domains as network requirements evolve to keep pace with increasingly demanding AI workloads," Boujelbene added. "The latest example is scale-in networking, designed to address the new requirements of agentic AI, while innovations such as CPO will help overcome growing bandwidth, power, and scalability challenges." ® Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Darkbloom, which allowed Mac users to rent out their GPUs for AI inference, warns of low payouts
2026-10-30

Darkbloom, which allowed Mac users to rent out their GPUs for AI inference, warns of low payouts

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Declining token prices have prompted Darkbloom, a distributed inference network, to reassess its business plans. Darkbloom, a project from Seattle-based tech biz Eigen Labs, has developed software that allows anyone with a sufficiently capable macOS device to rent their GPU through the Darkbloom network for interference jobs. In return, they get paid. Through this network, 800 or so participants – including as of two days ago this reporter – have been able to collect a few cents to a few dollars per day by having their hardware handle AI model prompts submitted through OpenRouter, an AI model gateway. In theory, someone running one of Apple's recently released Mac minis with an M5 Pro chip and 64 GB of RAM could earn as much as $192 per month handling incoming inference jobs, per the company's estimate. That assumes full 24/7 utilization during this period on a sought-after model like Qwen3.6 35B A3B, which sells for $0.065/M input tokens, $0.75/M output tokens. But not every network participant has fully surrendered their Mac hardware to the network. The median earnings of the 50 people on the leaderboard amount to about $5.26 per day, $159.86 per month, or $1,918.50 per year. So the best case estimate presented by Darkbloom looks plausible. But it may not be sustainable. The price of tokens – the unit of AI work – has declined, reportedly down 54 percent since May. Ramp's AI Token Price Index, which tracks the price of tokens from Anthropic and OpenAI, shows similar slippage. For Anthropic, the price per million tokens went from $2.65 on May 1, 2026 to $0.62 on October 22, 2026, a decline of 77 percent. In a message shared with Darkbloom participants on the company's Slack channel, Kydo, one of the Eigen Labs researchers working on the distributed inference network, celebrated the network's processing of its trillionth token while attempting to moderate expectations about the project's viability. "Earnings have been low," said Kydo. "For most providers, I don't expect that it is going to recover soon." Pointing to the computational prowess of DeekSeek's DeepInfer framework, he explained that structural changes in the inference market have affected the supply of tokens. "Data centers have become cheaper," he said. "Output per NVIDIA cluster is up ~20x this year, and their margins are wide enough to cut prices 90 percent and still profit at 50 percent." The flood of tokens in turn has washed away the price advantage Darkbloom had previously. "The market caught up to our price," Kydo explained. "We could go lower, but then most of you would earn even less nothing." Finally, he said, the inference routing market is changing, with OpenRouter moving away from price-based routing and putting more emphasis on decode speed, quantization, and prefill speed. For network participants with older or less powerful Mac hardware – those without top of the line Max or Ultra Apple Silicon – "inference won't be worth running," he said. Kydo went on to outline how Darkbloom aims to adapt to the commoditized token market. This involves analyzing model demand to steer participants toward more sought-after models, and looking at ways to provide other kinds of compute work, like distributed CI/CD jobs. For Mac hardware, the focus now appears to be on clusters of high-end Mac Studios (now available with up to 512 GB of RAM), using Apple's Remote Direct Memory Access (RDMA) over Thunderbolt. The CI/CD work, based on the observation that AI agents now make pull requests faster than CI/CD pipelines can handle them, will require sandboxing — something the biz expects to begin testing soon. And Darkbloom also aims to improve the performance of its network (in terms of time to first token) and to look for other token buyers beyond OpenRouter. "The next stretch will be a fight, and I expect many machines will leave and Slack will get quieter," Kydo concluded. "But those who stay will be first in line when new earning opportunities come along. We are a driven team and well capitalized. We will not give up." ® Experimental orbital datacenter prototype puts its TPU to work producing poetry Labeled in black on black - controls for a galactic hyper-hearse or ill-advised Windows theme? Happy Pikmin Day Eve

The Verge | 2026-10-31

Nintendo is celebrating the first annual Pikmin Day by debuting a new series of short films. Nintendo is celebrating the first annual Pikmin Day by debuting a new series of short films. This morning, Nintendo announced that tomorrow, November 1st, would be the first annual Pikmin Day. As part of the short Pikmin Day Direct, Shigeru Miyamoto shared new info about Pikmin 4 for the Switch 2, as well as an update for the mobile app Pikmin Bloom, and announced the Hello! Pikmin series of shorts. Players will be able to use the Switch 2’s built-in microphone to issue voice commands to Oatchi, the space dog. Pikmin 4 will also have a Dandori Academy mode that will test players’ skills in specific challenges. There will be limited tests on Friday, Saturday, and Sunday, complete with leaderboards where players can compare stats. Pikmin Bloom will be getting a new feature called Secret Caves, where players can win treasure by battling enemies and exploring said caves. Lastly, Miyamoto showed the first installment of a new series of shorts called Hello! Pikmin. A second episode will premiere tomorrow, with a third later in November. The series will then debut one short per month starting in December. The shorts build on the success of Close to You, a Pikmin animation released last year.
